    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.1

    ↗
    శ్రీభగవానువాచ బద్ధో ముక్త ఇతి వ్యాఖ్యా గుణతో మే న వస్తుత: । గుణస్య మాయామూలత్వాన్న మే మోక్షో న బన్ధనమ్ ॥ ౧ ॥
    śrī-bhagavān uvāca
    [shree-bhagavaan uvaacha]
    baddho mukta iti vyākhyā
    [baddho mukta iti vyaakhyaa]
    guṇato me na vastutaḥ
    [gunato me na vastutah]
    guṇasya māyā-mūlatvān
    [gunasya maayaa-moolatvaan]
    na me mokṣo na bandhanam
    [na me moksho na bandhanam]
    Word-for-word
    śrī-bhagavān uvāca — the Supreme Personality of Godhead said baddhaḥ — in bondage muktaḥ — liberated iti — thus vyākhyā — the explanation of the living entity guṇataḥ — due to the modes of material nature me — which are My potency na — not vastutaḥ — in reality guṇasya — of the modes of material nature māyā — My illusory energy mūlatvāt — because of being the cause na — not me — of Me mokṣaḥ — liberation na — nor bandhanam — bondage.

    bondage.
    The Supreme Personality of Godhead said: My dear Uddhava, due to the influence of the material modes of nature, which are under My control, the living entity is sometimes designated as conditioned and sometimes as liberated. In fact, however, the soul is never really bound up or liberated, and since I am the Supreme Lord of māyā, which is the cause of the modes of nature, I also am never to be considered liberated or in bondage.
    Purport

    In this chapter the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Kṛṣṇa, explains the different characteristics of conditioned and liberated life, the symptoms by which one can recognize saintly persons, and the various processes of devotional service to the Lord. In the previous chapter, Uddhava inquired from the Lord how conditioned and liberated life are possible. The Lord now replies that Uddhava’s question is somewhat superficial, since the pure spirit soul is never entangled in the material energy of the Lord. The living entity imagines a false connection with the three modes of nature and accepts the material body as the self. The living entity therefore suffers the consequences of his own imagination, just as one suffers the illusory activities of a dream. This does not indicate that the material world is illusory in the sense that it is nonexistent. The material world is certainly real, being the potency of the Personality of Godhead, and the living entity, being the superior potency of God, is also real. But the living entity’s dream of being part and parcel of the material world is an illusion that drags him into the contradictory state called material conditioned life. The living entity is never actually baddha, or bound up, since he merely imagines a false connection with the material world.

    Because there is ultimately no permanent connection between the living entity and matter, there is no actual liberation. The living entity, being eternally transcendental to the inferior material energy of the Lord, is eternally liberated. Lord Kṛṣṇa reveals that in one sense the living entity is factually not bound up and thus cannot be liberated. But in another sense, the terms bondage and liberation can be conveniently applied to indicate the particular situation of the individual soul, who is the marginal potency of the Lord. Although the individual soul is never actually bound to matter, he suffers the reactions of material nature because of false identification, and thus the term baddha, or “bound up,” may be used to indicate the nature of a living entity’s experience within the inferior energy of the Lord. Since baddha describes a false situation, freedom from such a false situation may also be described as mokṣa, or liberation. Therefore the terms bondage and liberation are acceptable if one understands that such terms only refer to temporary situations created by illusion and do not refer to the ultimate nature of the living entity. In this verse Lord Kṛṣṇa states, guṇasya māyā-mūlatvān na me mokṣo na bandhanam: the terms liberation and bondage can never be applied to the Supreme Personality of Godhead, since He is the Absolute Truth and the supreme controller of everything. Lord Kṛṣṇa is eternally the supreme transcendental entity, and He can never be bound by illusion. It is the duty of the illusory potency of the Personality of Godhead to attract the living entities to ignorance by creating the false impression of a blissful existence separate from Lord Kṛṣṇa. The illusory conception of existence apart from the Personality of Godhead is called māyā, or material illusion. Since Lord Kṛṣṇa is the supreme absolute controller of māyā, there is no possibility that māyā could have any influence over the Personality of Godhead. Thus the term bandhanam, or “bondage,” cannot be applied to the eternal, blissful and omniscient Personality of Godhead. The term mokṣa, or “liberation,” indicating freedom from bandhana, is equally irrelevant to the Lord.

    Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura has commented on this verse as follows. The Supreme Personality of Godhead is endowed with great spiritual potencies. Because of mundane concoction, the conditioned soul imagines that the Absolute Truth is devoid of variegated spiritual potencies by which He may enjoy blissful life. Although the living entity is the spiritual potency of the Lord, he is presently situated in the inferior, illusory potency, and by engaging in mental speculation he becomes bound in conditioned life. Liberation means that the living entity should transfer himself to the spiritual potency of the Lord, which can be divided into three categories — hlādinī, the potency of bliss; sandhinī, the potency of eternal existence; and saṁvit, the potency of omniscience. Since the Personality of Godhead is eternally endowed with a pure existence of bliss and knowledge, He is never conditioned or liberated. The living entity, however, being entangled in the Lord’s material potency, is sometimes conditioned and sometimes liberated.

    The neutral, original state of the three modes of nature is called māyā. When the three modes of nature interact, one of them will become powerful, subordinating the other two modes until another mode becomes prominent. In this way, the three can be distinguished in their variety of manifestation. Although the threefold material potency expands from the Personality of Godhead, the Lord Himself in His personal form is the actual abode of the three spiritual potencies, namely eternality, bliss and knowledge. If one desires to become free from the entanglement of conditioned life within the material sky, called the kingdom of māyā, one must come to the spiritual sky, wherein the living entities are filled with bliss, possess eternal spiritual bodies and engage in the loving devotional service of the Lord. By developing one’s eternal, spiritual form in the loving service of the Lord, one immediately transcends the duality of conditioned life and impersonal liberation and can directly experience the spiritual potencies of the Lord. At that time there is no possibility of false identification with the material world.

    Realizing oneself to be eternal spirit soul, the living entity can understand that he is never truly connected to matter, because he is part of the superior energy of the Lord. Therefore, both material bondage and liberation are ultimately meaningless within the reality of the spiritual sky. The living entity is the marginal potency of the Lord and should exercise his free will to engage in the pure devotional service of the Lord. By reviving one’s eternal, spiritual body one can understand oneself to be a minute particle of the spiritual potency of the Lord. In other words, the living entity is a minute particle of eternity, bliss and omniscience, and thus in full Kṛṣṇa consciousness there is no possibility of his being carried away by the illusion of the three modes of nature. In conclusion, it may be stated that the individual living entity is never actually entangled in matter and is thus not liberated, although his illusory state may be accurately described as entangled and liberated. On the other hand, the Supreme Personality of Godhead is eternally situated in His own spiritual potencies and can never be described as being bound up, and thus there is no meaning to the concept of the Lord’s freeing Himself from such a nonexistent condition.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The Lord said: One cannot actually say that there is bondage or liberation due to the guṇas controlled by me. Because the relationship with the gūnas arises only by avidyā or ignorance, there is no real bondage, and consequently no liberation. That is my opinion. In the Eleventh Chapter the Lord explains the difference between the liberated and conditioned jīvas, the qualities of the devotee and the aṅgas of bhakti. At the end of the last chapter Uddhava inquired about the liberated and conditioned jīvas. Now the Lord explains that the question does not arise. It is impossible to say that a jīva is bound by the guṇas which are dependent on the Lord, or that the jīva is liberated from them. Why? It arises by the inconceivable energy called avidyā, related to the guṇas. Thus it is only an appearance of a relationship with the guṇas, body and senses, a relationship difficult to accomplish. This is my opinion. There is no bondage, and therefore there is no liberation, since there is no bondage from which to be liberated.
    Personal Guidance
    Bondage and liberation are descriptions of the soul's illusion under the guṇas — Kṛṣṇa, master of māyā, is untouched by either, and so are you in truth.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice the next time you say "I am stuck" or "I am free" — pause and ask which guṇa is speaking through that self-description.
    • When a wave of sattva, rajas, or tamas rises today, name it aloud as māyā's movement, not as your identity.
    • Offer one act of devotional service to Kṛṣṇa as the supreme controller of māyā, trusting His shelter rather than your own efforts to "get free."
    Reflection Questions
    • Where am I treating my conditioned situation as the ultimate fact about me, instead of as a dream-state under the guṇas?
    • If both bondage and liberation are temporary descriptions, what is left as my actual relationship with Kṛṣṇa right now?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • When an exam result, a breakup, or a social media post makes you feel "this is who I am now" — pause and recognize that as a guṇa-shaped story, not your soul.
    • Refuse the campus pressure to define yourself by achievements or failures; remind yourself daily that the eternal soul is neither the topper nor the dropout.
    • Pick one short verse or name of Kṛṣṇa and chant it before opening your phone, anchoring identity in Him before the modes of nature start narrating you.
    householder
    • When stress at work or with the children makes you feel "hopelessly stuck," name it as rajas or tamas tightening, and consciously offer the situation to Kṛṣṇa before reacting.
    • Refuse to treat career success or family security as your actual liberation; keep them in their place as duties, while reserving true shelter for Kṛṣṇa.
    • Lead one short family practice — a verse, a prayer, prasāda offered together — so that the home itself becomes a place where the soul's real situation is remembered.
    senior
    • As the body declines, refuse the inner script that says "I am being slowly bound by old age"; meet each limitation with the conviction that the soul is untouched.
    • Loosen one attachment this week — a possession, a role, an old grievance — as a concrete act of stepping back from the false identification the verse describes.
    • Mentor a younger devotee or family member by sharing, in plain language, how you now see past episodes of "bondage" and "freedom" in your life as movements of the guṇas under Kṛṣṇa's control.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Hearing "the soul is never truly bound" and using it to excuse continued sense-indulgence, as if sādhana no longer matters.
    • Sliding into impersonalism — concluding that since bondage is illusory, Kṛṣṇa Himself must be a featureless absolute rather than the personal Lord of māyā.
    • Mistaking philosophical clarity about non-bondage for actual freedom, while still identifying moment-by-moment with the body and mind.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a recent experience where you felt either "trapped" or "free" — and examine together which guṇa was shaping that feeling.
    • Kṛṣṇa calls Uddhava's question superficial. Where in your own spiritual life have you been asking superficial questions, and what deeper question is the verse pointing you toward?
    • If liberation is not really "escape" but transfer to Kṛṣṇa's spiritual potency, how does that change what you are practicing for?
    Role Play

    One student plays a sincere seeker who declares, "I have realized I am the eternal soul, never bound, so I no longer need to follow any sādhana." Another student plays a Gauḍīya friend who, drawing on this verse, must lovingly show why "never bound" does not cancel the need to surrender to Kṛṣṇa personally and engage in devotional service.

    Objections & Responses
    • If the soul is never really bound, this whole spiritual struggle is a fiction — why bother with sādhana at all?
      The bondage is illusory, but the suffering under that illusion is real, like the distress of a dream. Sādhana is not the manufacture of a freedom that does not exist; it is the waking up from a false identification, performed in loving service to Kṛṣṇa, who is the actual shelter from māyā.
    • This sounds like Advaita — bondage and liberation are both illusions, so the Absolute must be impersonal.
      The verse says the opposite. Kṛṣṇa speaks as a person — "I am the Supreme Lord of māyā" — and explains that He is untouched by bondage or liberation precisely because He is eternally situated in His own spiritual potencies of hlādinī, sandhinī, and saṁvit. The dissolution is of our false connection with matter, not of His personal, blissful form.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.2

    ↗
    శోకమోహౌ సుఖం దు:ఖం దేహాపత్తిశ్చ మాయయా । స్వప్నో యథాత్మన: ఖ్యాతి: సంసృతిర్న తు వాస్తవీ ॥ ౨ ॥
    śoka-mohau sukhaṁ duḥkhaṁ
    [shoka-mohau sukham duhkham]
    dehāpattiś ca māyayā
    [dehaapattish cha maayayaa]
    svapno yathātmanaḥ khyātiḥ
    [svapno yathaatmanah khyaatih]
    saṁsṛtir na tu vāstavī
    [samsritir na tu vaastavee]

    śoka — lamentation mohau — and illusion sukham — happiness duḥkham — distress deha-āpattiḥ — accepting a material body ca — also māyayā — by the influence of māyā svapnaḥ — a dream yathā — just as ātmanaḥ — of the intelligence khyātiḥ — merely an idea saṁsṛtiḥ — material existence na — is not tu — indeed vāstavī — real.

    Just as a dream is merely a creation of one’s intelligence but has no actual substance, similarly, material lamentation, illusion, happiness, distress and the acceptance of the material body under the influence of māyā are all creations of My illusory energy. In other words, material existence has no essential reality.
    Purport

    The word deha-āpattiḥ indicates that the living entity falsely identifies himself with the external material body and thus transmigrates from one body to another. Āpatti also indicates great suffering or misfortune. Because of such false identification under the influence of illusion, the living entity experiences the miserable symptoms described here. Māyā means the false concept that anything can exist without Lord Kṛṣṇa or for any purpose other than the pleasure of the Supreme Lord. Although the conditioned living entities are trying to enjoy material sense gratification, the result is always painful, and such painful experiences turn the conditioned soul back toward the Supreme Personality of Godhead. In other words, the ultimate purpose of the material creation is to bring the living entity back to the loving devotional service of the Lord. Therefore, even the sufferings of the material world may be seen as the transcendental mercy of the Personality of Godhead. The conditioned soul, imagining that material objects are meant for his personal enjoyment, bitterly laments the loss of such objects. In this verse, the example is given of a dream in which the material intelligence creates many illusory objects. Similarly, our polluted material consciousness creates the false impression of material sense gratification, but this phantasmagoria, being devoid of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, has no real existence. By surrendering to polluted material consciousness, the living entity is afflicted with innumerable troubles. The only solution is to see Lord Kṛṣṇa within everything and everything within Lord Kṛṣṇa. Thus, one understands that Lord Kṛṣṇa is the supreme enjoyer, the proprietor of everything and the well-wishing friend of all living beings.

    In material illusion there is no understanding of one’s eternal, spiritual body, nor is there knowledge of the Absolute Truth. Therefore, material existence, even in its most sophisticated or pious form, is always foolishness. One should not misunderstand the example of the dream to mean that the material world has no real existence. Material nature is the manifestation of the Lord’s external potency, just as the spiritual sky is the manifestation of the Lord’s internal potency. Although material objects are subject to transformation and thus have no permanent existence, the material energy is real because it comes from the supreme reality, Lord Kṛṣṇa. It is only our false acceptance of the material body as the factual self and our foolish dream that the material world is meant for our pleasure that have no real existence. They are merely mental concoctions. One should cleanse oneself of material designations and wake up to the all-pervading reality of the Personality of Godhead, Lord Kṛṣṇa.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Just as a dream is merely a creation of one’s intelligence but has no actual substance, similarly, material lamentation, illusion, happiness, distress and the acceptance of another material body take place by avidyā and are not based on reality. He explains how bondage is illusory. Dehāpattiḥ means “acceptance of another body after giving up a body,” or “death of the body.” Acceptance of a body takes place by avidyā (māyayā), by a relationship with a conditioning produced by māyā. This conditioning arises only from identification of the jīva with his subtle body or antaḥkaraṇa. Because of this identification one accepts the qualities of the subtle body—lamentation, illusion, happiness and distress—as one’s own qualities. Thus saṁsāra, consisting of identity with lamentation and illusion, is not based on reality. Though lamentation and illusion actually exist as the creations of māyā, their relation with the jīva is false, produced by avidyā. This is like the transformation (khyātiḥ) of one’s intelligence to produce a dream, which is false.
    Personal Guidance
    Material lamentation, joy, distress, and bodily identification are dream-stuff spun by māyā — only Kṛṣṇa and service to Him are substantial.
    Practical Applications
    • When a wave of grief or elation hits today, name it aloud as māyā's dream-content and refuse to let it dictate your next decision.
    • Each morning, before checking your phone, recall that the body you just woke into is borrowed and offer the day's actions to Kṛṣṇa.
    • Notice one painful loss you are nursing and re-read it as Kṛṣṇa's mercy turning you back toward Him, not as a personal catastrophe.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which 'losses' am I lamenting as if they were real, when the verse says they are dream-objects of a polluted intelligence?
    • Where am I still treating this body as the factual self, and what daily habit reinforces that misidentification?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • When an exam result or a breakup feels like the end of the world, write down the feeling and label it dream-content of borrowed intelligence before you post about it.
    • Audit your social feed for one week and notice how each scroll reinforces the belief that this body and its image are the real you — then cut the worst offender.
    • Pick one peer-pressure moment this week and refuse it on the explicit ground that you are not this body and its reputation is not your eternal self.
    householder
    • When a financial setback or a child's misbehavior triggers lamentation, pause and ask whether you are reacting as the soul or as the proprietor of a dream-household.
    • Schedule a weekly family conversation in which you read this verse together and name one shared anxiety as māyā's projection, then offer the week's work to Kṛṣṇa.
    • Refuse to make a major career or purchase decision today out of fear of losing a material 'fruit' — act from duty to Kṛṣṇa, not from dread of dream-loss.
    senior
    • Each evening, recall the bodies you have already passed through — child, youth, parent — and rest in the continuity of the soul that watched them all dissolve.
    • Loosen grip on one possession or role you have been clutching, and hand it deliberately to a younger person as practice for the final letting-go.
    • When physical pain or news of a peer's death arises, receive it as Kṛṣṇa's mercy turning you homeward, and speak of Him to one person before the day ends.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Twisting 'the world is dream-like' into nihilism or apathy, ignoring that the material energy itself is real because it comes from Kṛṣṇa.
    • Using 'it's all māyā' as a slogan to dodge family duties, work, and sādhana rather than to dismantle false bodily identification.
    • Drifting into impersonalism — treating the verse as proof of an empty Absolute, when it points to surrender to Kṛṣṇa as the supreme enjoyer.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a recent moment of intense happiness or distress — in what specific way was it a dream-creation of intelligence, and in what way did it feel undeniably real?
    • If material existence is unreal in essence yet the material energy is real because it comes from Kṛṣṇa, how does that change how you treat your job, body, and bank account this week?
    • Where do you disagree with the verse's claim that even pious material life is 'foolishness' — and what would it cost you to actually accept it?
    Role Play

    One student has just lost a job they loved and is grieving openly; the other is a friend trying to console them without slipping into either cheap 'it's all māyā' dismissal or worldly sympathy that reinforces bodily identification — show how a Gauḍīya friend would speak.

    Objections & Responses
    • Calling suffering a 'dream' is cruel — tell that to someone in a hospital bed or a refugee camp.
      The verse does not deny that the experience is felt; it diagnoses why it bites so deeply — because we have accepted a false self. Prabhupāda explicitly says material energy is real; only our identification with the body and our claim of proprietorship are the dream. Real compassion means serving the eternal soul of the sufferer, not merely sedating the body's discomfort.
    • This sounds like classic Advaita — the world is illusion, only the formless Absolute is real.
      Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavism rejects that reading. Kṛṣṇa, the speaker, is a person, and He calls māyā His own energy — real, but external. What is illusory is the conditioned soul's claim that material objects exist for his enjoyment apart from Kṛṣṇa. Waking up means seeing Kṛṣṇa within everything and everything within Kṛṣṇa, not dissolving into a featureless light.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.3

    ↗
    విద్యావిద్యే మమ తనూ విద్ధ్యుద్ధవ శరీరిణామ్ । మోక్షబన్ధకరీ ఆద్యే మాయయా మే వినిర్మితే ॥ ౩ ॥
    vidyāvidye mama tanū
    [vidyaavidye mama tanoo]
    viddhy uddhava śarīriṇām
    [viddhy uddhava shareerinaam]
    mokṣa-bandha-karī ādye
    [moksha-bandha-karee aadye]
    māyayā me vinirmite
    [maayayaa me vinirmite]

    vidyā — knowledge avidye — and ignorance mama — My tanū — manifested energies viddhi — please understand uddhava — O Uddhava śarīriṇām — of the embodied living entities mokṣa — liberation bandha — bondage karī — causing ādye — original, eternal māyayā — by the potency me — My vinirmite — produced.

    vinirmite
    produced.
    O Uddhava, both knowledge and ignorance, being products of māyā, are expansions of My potency. Both knowledge and ignorance are beginningless and perpetually award liberation and bondage to embodied living beings.
    Purport

    By the expansion of vidyā, or knowledge, a conditioned soul is liberated from the clutches of māyā, and similarly, by the expansion of avidyā, or ignorance, the conditioned soul is driven further into illusion and bondage. Both knowledge and ignorance are products of the mighty potency of the Personality of Godhead. The living being is bound by illusion when he considers himself the proprietor of the subtle and gross material bodies. According to Śrīla Jīva Gosvāmī the living entity may be designated as jīva-māyā, whereas matter is called guṇa-māyā. The living entity places his living potency ( jīva-māyā ) in the grip of the mundane qualitative potency ( guṇa-māyā ) and falsely dreams that he is part and parcel of the material world. Such an artificial mixture is called illusion or ignorance. When all of the Lord’s potencies are correctly perceived in their proper categories, the living entity is liberated from material bondage and returns to his blissful eternal residence in the spiritual sky.

    The Supreme Personality of Godhead is not different from His potencies, yet He is always above them as the supreme controller. The Supreme Personality of Godhead may be designated as mukta, or liberated, only to indicate that He is eternally free from material contamination and never to indicate that the Lord has been freed from actual entanglement in a material situation. According to Śrīla Madhvācārya, vidyā indicates the goddess of fortune, the internal potency of the Lord, whereas avidyā indicates Durgā, the external potency of the Lord. Ultimately, however, the Personality of Godhead can transform His potencies according to His own desire, as explained by Śrīla Prabhupāda in his commentary on Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam (1.3.34) , “Because the Lord is the absolute Transcendence, all of His forms, names, pastimes, attributes, associates and energies are identical with Him. His transcendental energy acts according to His omnipotency. The same energy acts as His external, internal and marginal energies, and by His omnipotency He can perform anything and everything through the agency of any of the above energies. He can turn the external energy into internal by His will.”

    Śrīla Śrīdhara Svāmī notes in this regard that although the Lord has explained in the first verse of this chapter that the living entity is never actually in bondage, and therefore never actually liberated, one may apply the terms bondage and liberation if one remembers that the living entity is eternally a transcendental fragment of the Personality of Godhead. Further, one should not misinterpret the words māyayā me vinirmite to indicate that both material bondage and liberation are temporary states, being creations of the potency of the Lord. Therefore, the term ādye, or “primeval and eternal,” is used in this verse. The vidyā and avidyā potencies of the Lord are stated to be creations of māyā because they carry out the functions of the Lord’s potencies. The vidyā potency engages the living entities in the Lord’s pastimes, whereas the avidyā potency engages the living entities in forgetting the Lord and merging into darkness. Actually, both knowledge and ignorance are eternal alternatives of the marginal potency of the Lord, and in this sense it is not incorrect to state that the living entity is either eternally conditioned or eternally liberated. The term vinirmite, or “produced,” in this case indicates that the Lord expands His own energy as knowledge and ignorance, which display the functions of the Lord’s internal and external potencies. Such potential exhibitions may appear and disappear in different times, places and circumstances, but material bondage and spiritual freedom are eternal options of the marginal potency of the Lord.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    O Uddhava! Understand that vidyā and avidyā are my śaktis. They are created by my māyā, are without beginning, and create liberation and bondage for the living beings. “What is this avidyā, by which the jīva develops a false relationship with the guṇas?” Both vidyā and avidyā are my śaktis (tanū), by which liberation and bondage are produced. Vidyā produces liberation and avidyā produces bondage for the embodied beings. These are created by my great śakti, māyā. Moreover, because they are aspects of māyā, metaphorically, they are said to be “created” by māyā. But they are actually without beginning (ādye). na tasya kālāvayavaiḥ pariṇāmādayo guṇāḥ anādy anantam avyaktaṁ nityaṁ kāraṇam avyayam Prakṛti is not subject to the six kinds of transformation caused by the influence of time. Rather, it has no birth, no death, no existence, no increase, no change, and no decrease. It is the cause of the universe. SB 12.4.19 Medical scriptures also say puṁso’ sti prakṛtir nityā: the prakṛti of the Lord is eternal. Like māyā, her modes, vidyā and avidyā, are eternal. Māyā thus has three modes: pradhāna, avidyā and vidyā. Pradhāna creates the coverings (upadhi—subtle and gross bodies), which are real. Avidyā creates the imposition of the body (adhyāsa), which is false. Vidyā destroys the false identity. These are the three products of the three aspects of māyā.
    Personal Guidance
    Knowledge and ignorance are both Kṛṣṇa's potencies — one binds you, one liberates you; today, choose which of His energies you let act on you.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice one moment today when you claim proprietorship of body, talent, or possession — and silently re-attribute it to Kṛṣṇa as His potency working through you.
    • Refuse to treat ignorance as merely personal failure; recognize it as avidyā operating, and counter it with one deliberate act of hearing or chanting about Kṛṣṇa.
    • Spend ten minutes today studying scripture under guidance — let vidyā, His internal potency, act on you instead of drifting in your own opinions.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where am I currently letting avidyā quietly reinforce my sense of being the proprietor — of my career, family, or even my spiritual progress?
    • If both knowledge and ignorance are Kṛṣṇa's potencies and beginningless, what does that mean for how I should treat my own past slips into illusion?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Audit your feed for one hour: which posts feed avidyā (envy, comparison, proprietorship over your image) and which could feed vidyā (hearing about Kṛṣṇa, association with serious devotees)?
    • When a low grade or rejection comes, refuse the story "I am a failure" — recognize that as ahaṅkāra under avidyā, and counter it by reading one verse with purport tonight.
    • In your friend group, notice the moment you start performing your "authentic self" for approval; pause and ask which of Kṛṣṇa's two potencies that performance is serving.
    householder
    • In your next family money decision, name out loud which choice is shaped by proprietorship ("my" savings, "my" children's future) and which treats the resources as Kṛṣṇa's energy passing through you.
    • Pick one recurring household quarrel and trace it to mistaking guṇa-māyā (the body's moods, the home's tensions) for jīva-māyā (the actual person you live with); respond to the person, not the qualities.
    • Build a non-negotiable fifteen-minute slot — before children wake or after they sleep — where vidyā gets to act on you through scripture, so the rest of the day is not pure avidyā by default.
    senior
    • Review the bodies you have already passed through — child, youth, householder — and identify which transitions you misread as annihilations because avidyā was operating; let that recognition steady you for the next transition.
    • When you mentor a younger devotee, do not transmit accumulated opinions; transmit vidyā by pointing them, verse by verse, back to Kṛṣṇa's words and His servants.
    • Begin loosening one attachment this month — a possession, a role, an entitlement — not as renunciation theater, but as a practical refusal to let avidyā keep dressing the body as your proprietorship.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Concluding that since both vidyā and avidyā come from Kṛṣṇa, it doesn't matter which one you cultivate — collapsing the very distinction the verse insists on.
    • Treating "knowledge" as accumulated information about Vedic topics rather than the active internal potency that engages one in Kṛṣṇa's service.
    • Reading māyayā me vinirmite as proof that bondage and liberation are temporary illusions, ignoring the word ādye that establishes them as primeval and real options.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a recent decision where you can clearly see avidyā was operating — and one where vidyā was. What distinguished them in lived experience, not just in theory?
    • If Kṛṣṇa is the source of both potencies, does that make Him responsible for your bondage? Discuss honestly, then test your answer against the verse's framing.
    • Where in your life do you mix jīva-māyā with guṇa-māyā — placing your living potency into material qualities — and what would unmixing actually look like this week?
    Role Play

    A long-time devotee tells a newcomer: "Don't worry about studying so much — knowledge and ignorance are both māyā anyway, just chant." Another student in the room has read SB 11.11.3 this morning. Role-play the conversation: how does the second student lovingly correct the misreading without dismissing chanting, using the verse's actual distinction between vidyā and avidyā as Kṛṣṇa's two potencies?

    Objections & Responses
    • If knowledge and ignorance are both products of māyā, then liberation itself is just another illusion — why bother pursuing it?
      The verse anticipates this with the word ādye — primeval and eternal. Śrīla Śrīdhara Svāmī clarifies that vidyā and avidyā are eternal alternatives of Kṛṣṇa's marginal potency, not temporary products to be transcended. Bondage and liberation are real options for the jīva, not stage-illusions to wave away.
    • Calling one potency "the goddess of fortune" and another "Durgā" sounds like mythological dressing for what's really just psychology — wisdom versus delusion.
      Madhvācārya's identification is not decoration; it points to the personal character of Kṛṣṇa's energies. Vidyā is not an abstract state of mind but His internal potency drawing you toward Him; avidyā is His external potency, personified, keeping you turned away. Treating them as personal forces is what lets you actually surrender to one rather than manage the other.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.4

    ↗
    ఏకస్యైవ మమాంశస్య జీవస్యైవ మహామతే । బన్ధోఽస్యావిద్యయానాదిర్విద్యయా చ తథేతర: ॥ ౪ ॥
    ekasyaiva mamāṁśasya
    [ekasyaiva mamaamshasya]
    jīvasyaiva mahā-mate
    [jeevasyaiva mahaa-mate]
    bandho ’syāvidyayānādir
    [bandho ’syaavidyayaanaadir]
    vidyayā ca tathetaraḥ
    [vidyayaa cha tathetarah]
    Word-for-word
    ekasya — of the one eva — certainly mama — My aṁśasya — part and parcel jīvasya — of the living entity eva — certainly mahā-mate — O most intelligent one bandhaḥ — bondage asya — of him avidyayā — by ignorance anādiḥ — beginningless vidyayā — by knowledge ca — and tathā — similarly itaraḥ — the opposite of bondage, liberation.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    ekasyaiva mamāṁśasya
    ekasya
    of the one

    part and parcel
    jīvasyaiva mahā-mate
    jīvasya
    of the living entity

    mahā-mate

    bandho ’syāvidyayānādir
    bandhaḥ
    bondage
    asya
    of him

    the opposite of bondage, liberation.
    O most intelligent Uddhava, the living entity, called jīva, is part and parcel of Me, but due to ignorance he has been suffering in material bondage since time immemorial. By knowledge, however, he can be liberated.
    Purport

    Just as the sun reveals itself through its own light or covers itself by creating clouds, the Personality of Godhead reveals and covers Himself by knowledge and ignorance, which are expansions of His potency. As stated in Bhagavad-gītā (7.5) :

    apareyam itas tv anyāṁ prakṛtiṁ viddhi me parām jīva-bhūtāṁ mahā-bāho yayedaṁ dhāryate jagat

    “Besides this inferior nature, O mighty-armed Arjuna, there is a superior energy of Mine, consisting of all living entities who are struggling with material nature and are sustaining the universe.” Śrīla Prabhupāda states in connection with this verse: “The Supreme Lord Kṛṣṇa is the only controller, and all living entities are controlled by Him. These living entities are His superior energy because the quality of their existence is one and the same with the Supreme, but they are never equal to the Lord in quantity of power.”

    Because of quantitative inferiority of potency, the living entity becomes covered by māyā and is again liberated by surrendering to the Lord. The word aṁśa, or “part and parcel,” is also mentioned in Bhagavad-gītā (15.7) : mamaivāṁśo jīva-loke jīva-bhūtaḥ sanātanaḥ. The living entity is aṁśa, or a minute particle, and therefore subject to liberation and bondage. As stated in the Viṣṇu Purāṇa:

    viṣṇu-śaktiḥ parā proktā kṣetrajṣākhyā tathā parā avidyā-karma-saṁjṣānyā tṛtīyā śaktir iṣyate

    “The Supreme Personality of Godhead, Viṣṇu, possesses His superior internal potency as well as the potency called kṣetrajṣā śakti. This kṣetrajṣā śakti is also spiritual potency, but it is sometimes covered by the third, or material, potency called ignorance. Thus because of the various stages of covering, the second, or marginal, potency is manifested in different evolutionary phases.”

    Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura has written that the living entity has been executing fruitive activities since time immemorial. Thus his conditioned life may be called beginningless. Such conditioned life, however, is not endless, since the living entity may achieve liberation through the loving devotional service of the Lord. Since the living entity may acquire liberation, Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura states that his liberated life begins at a certain point but is endless, because liberated life is understood to be eternal. In any case, one who has achieved the shelter of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Kṛṣṇa, may be understood to be eternally liberated, since such a person has entered into the eternal atmosphere of the spiritual sky. Since there is no material time in the spiritual sky, one who has achieved his eternal spiritual body on Lord Kṛṣṇa’s planet is not subject to the influence of time. His eternal blissful life with Kṛṣṇa is not designated in terms of material past, present and future and is therefore called eternal liberation. Material time is conspicuous by its absence in the spiritual sky, and every living entity there is eternally liberated, having attained the supreme situation. Such liberation can be achieved by vidyā, or perfect knowledge, which is understood in three phases called Brahman, Paramātmā and Bhagavān, as described in Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam. The ultimate phase of vidyā, or knowledge, is to understand the Supreme Personality of Godhead. In Bhagavad-gītā such knowledge is called rāja-vidyā, or the king of all knowledge, and it awards the supreme liberation.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    O intelligent Uddhava! The bondage of the jīva, who is my one part or taṭastha-śakti, is created by avidyā and is without beginning. By vidyā, he achieves liberation which has a beginning. Bondage of my śakti, the jīva, which is apparent only, is caused by my avidyā-śakti, which produces the imposition of the body, and liberation is produced by my vidyā-śakti which removes the imposition of the body. This is brought about under the influence of my śakti which functions for the pastime of creation and destruction of the universe. That is explained in this verse. Though the jīva is my part or aṁśa, it should be understood to be different from me (vibhinnāṁśa). [Note: Some forms of the Lord are also called aṁśa. Garbhodakaśāyi is an aṁśa of Mahāviṣṇu. ] I have said: apareyam itas tv anyāṁ prakṛtiṁ viddhi me parām | jīva-bhūtāṁ mahā-bāho yayedaṁ dhāryate jagat || This is my inferior energy. Understand my superior energy which is different from this inferior energy. It is the jīvas, by whom the inferior energy is employed for their enjoyment. BG 7.5 Though the jīva is my śakti, its nature as aṁśa should be understood from this statement: mamaivāṁśo jīva-loke jīva-bhūtaḥ sanātanaḥ | manaḥ-ṣaṣṭhānīndriyāṇi prakṛti-sthāni karṣati || This eternal jīva, one of my parts, drags with it the senses and the sixth sense called the mind, which are situated in the bewildering prakṛti. BG 15.7 “You have mentioned many living beings in the previous verse. Śruti also says there are many jīvas. Nityo nityānāṁ cetanaś cetanānam eko bahūnāṁ yo vidadhāti kāmān: the Lord is the chief eternal conscious entity among many eternal jīvas, and he alone maintains the many jīvas. (Kaṭha Upaniṣad 2.2.13) Why do you call the jīva “one” in this verse?” The jīva-śakti or taṭastha-śakti is one, but because of its many functions there are many jīvas. Similarly, the one external energy called māyā-śakti has two aspects avidyā and vidyā, which affect many jīvas by expansion into many functions. Just as all the expansions of māyā are simply called māyā, so all the expansions of the jīva-śakti are called jīva. The many expansions of the jīva-śakti and māyā-śakti should be understood to be eternal. The jīva is eternal, but by vidyā the jīva becomes liberated. It is said that when avidyā is destroyed the jīva attains liberation. But “destruction” simply means that avidyā ceases its influence on a particular jīva (since avidyā is eternal). Liberation or nirvāṇa means the jīva merges in Brahman. The jīva is not destroyed. Sāyujya means “joining with Brahman.” In that condition, the jīva’s svarūpa is not destroyed. viṣṇu-śaktiḥ parā proktā kṣetra-jṣākhyā tathā parā avidyā-karma-saṁjṣānyā tṛtīyā śaktir The Lord has a superior energy, another energy called the jīva and a third energy, the material energy, called avidyā-karma. Viṣṇu Purāṇa. 6.7.61 yayā kṣetra-śaktiḥ ……. ṣa tāratemyena vartate || [Note: The full text is : yayā kṣetrajṣaśaktiḥsā veṣṭitā nṛpasarvagā / saṁsāratāpānakhilānavāprotyatisaṁtatān // tayā tirohitatvācca śaktiḥ kṣetrajṣasaṁjṣitā / sarvabhūteṣu bhūpāla tāratamyena lakṣyate // ] The jīva-śakti exists in various conditions in various bodies by the māyā-śakti. From the statement of the Viṣṇu Purāṇa, it is understood that the jīva-śakti is generally controlled by māyā-śakti for accomplishing the pastime of creation of the universe. That is stated in the verse. The jīva is bound by avidyā. The avidyā of the jīva is beginningless because karma is beginningless. When liberation takes place, avidyā has an end for that particular jīva. Liberation, because it is produced, has a beginning. But because it is indestructible, it has no end.
    Personal Guidance
    You are Kṛṣṇa's aṁśa — beginninglessly bound by avidyā, but endlessly free the moment vidyā, surrender to Him, takes hold.
    Practical Applications
    • Today, notice one moment when you act as an independent controller and silently correct it: "I am His aṁśa, not the proprietor."
    • Spend ten minutes hearing or reading about Kṛṣṇa as a deliberate act of vidyā — treat it as the actual cause of your liberation, not a hobby.
    • Identify one avidyā-habit (a possessive thought, a sense indulgence, a false identification) and refuse it once today as a concrete withdrawal from material bondage.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where am I still treating my bondage as a personal accident rather than as anādi avidyā — and how does that excuse keep me from seriously taking up vidyā?
    • If liberation begins the moment I take shelter of Kṛṣṇa, what specifically am I waiting for before I begin?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • When peers pressure you to perform an identity online, name it silently: "This is avidyā dressing me up — I am Kṛṣṇa's aṁśa, not my follower count."
    • Before opening your phone or starting a study session, take thirty seconds to invoke vidyā: "Kṛṣṇa, let this hour move me toward You, not deeper into forgetting."
    • Pick one friendship where you are subtly trying to control outcomes (status, attention, romantic leverage) and consciously release the grip today as a small act of withdrawing from bondage.
    householder
    • In one decision today about money, work, or the children, act as a caretaker of Kṛṣṇa's energies rather than as the proprietor — and notice how the anxiety shifts.
    • Take ten minutes after the family is settled to study a single verse or purport as deliberate vidyā — treat it as more strategically important than the next professional skill.
    • When you next feel possessive about your spouse, child, or career role, silently re-frame: "These are Kṛṣṇa's, entrusted to me" — and let that re-frame, not willpower, loosen the grip.
    senior
    • Sit with one long-held attachment — a property, a reputation, an old grievance — and ask whether holding it is vidyā or avidyā at this stage; release accordingly.
    • Tell one younger person plainly, in your own words, what you have understood about being Kṛṣṇa's aṁśa — let your remaining years transmit vidyā rather than merely manage decline.
    • Each evening, formally place the day — its bodily aches, its forgetfulness, its remaining duties — at Kṛṣṇa's feet, practicing the surrender that the verse calls the actual end of bondage.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading "part and parcel" as equality with Kṛṣṇa in quantity of power, sliding into the impersonalist error this verse explicitly rules out.
    • Using "bondage is beginningless" as a fatalistic shrug — "it has always been this way" — instead of seizing the vidyā-side the verse equally offers.
    • Treating vidyā as mere information about the soul rather than as living surrender to Kṛṣṇa that culminates in Bhagavān-realization.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Kṛṣṇa says the jīva is His aṁśa, yet bound by avidyā since time immemorial — how do you hold "I belong to Him" and "I have been forgetting Him forever" in the same breath without despair?
    • Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura says bondage is beginningless but not endless, while liberation has a beginning but no end — what does this asymmetry change in how you plan your day, your year, your life?
    • Where in your life do you see avidyā operating not as obvious sin but as a respectable covering — career identity, family role, intellectual pride — and would the group agree it is avidyā?
    Role Play

    A close friend says, "I believe I am one with the divine — bondage is just an illusion I need to wake up from; I don't need to surrender to any particular Kṛṣṇa." Drawing only on SB 11.11.4 and its purport (aṁśa, quantitative inferiority, vidyā as surrender), respond in a way that is affectionate and clear, distinguishing the jīva as marginal potency from the Supreme, without lecturing.

    Objections & Responses
    • If my bondage is beginningless, then it is essentially built into me — why should I blame myself or strain for liberation that I never freely chose to lose?
      The verse does not assign blame; it diagnoses a condition. Avidyā is anādi, yes, but the same verse in the same breath offers vidyā as the equally available remedy. You are not asked to account for how you fell — you are asked what you will do now that Kṛṣṇa Himself has named the way out. Refusing the medicine because the disease is old is not humility; it is a second covering.
    • Saying the jīva is "part and parcel" of God sounds like a softer way of saying we are all God — why insist on a personal Kṛṣṇa above us?
      The verse and its purport are precise: the jīva is one in quality with Kṛṣṇa but never equal in quantity of power. We are His marginal potency, sometimes covered by the third, material potency. "Part and parcel" means belonging to Him, not being Him. The personalist line is what makes surrender, and therefore liberation, an actual relationship rather than a self-congratulation.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.5

    ↗
    అథ బద్ధస్య ముక్తస్య వైలక్షణ్యం వదామి తే । విరుద్ధధర్మిణోస్తాత స్థితయోరేకధర్మిణి ॥ ౫ ॥

    Thus, My dear Uddhava, in the same material body we find opposing characteristics, such as great happiness and misery. That is because both the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who is eternally liberated, as well as the conditioned soul are within the body. I shall now speak to you about their different characteristics.
    Purport

    In verse 36 of the previous chapter, Uddhava inquired about the different symptoms of liberated and conditioned life. Śrīla Śrīdhara Svāmī explains that the characteristics of bondage and liberation may be understood in two divisions — as the difference between the ordinary conditioned soul and the eternally liberated Personality of Godhead, or as the difference between conditioned and liberated living entities in the jīva category. The Lord will first explain the difference between the ordinary living entity and the Supreme Personality of Godhead, which may be understood as the difference between the controlled and the controller.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Hear about the difference between the jīva and Paramātmā. They display opposite qualities, though situated in one body. Uddhava asked about the qualities of the liberated jīva (SB 11.10.36). W e hear statements like “This jīva is in bondage and that jīva is liberated,” “The jīva is in bondage and Paramātmā is liberated,” or “That ātmā is without sin.” First hear about the difference between jīva and Paramātmā. This is explained in one and half verses. Two possessors of qualities with opposite natures (viruddha-dharmiṇoḥ)—lamentation and bliss-- are situated in one body as the controlled and the controller.
    Personal Guidance
    Two utterly different beings share this one body — the eternally liberated Lord and the conditioned jīva; know which one you are.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today the swing between great happiness and misery in your own body, and recognize it as the jīva's signature, not the Lord's.
    • Before acting, pause and ask which of the two residents in your heart you are consulting — the controller or the controlled.
    • Speak inwardly to Kṛṣṇa as a distinct Person seated beside you in this very body, not as a vague inner glow.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my day do I quietly assume I am the controller, forgetting the Personality of Godhead seated within the same body?
    • What opposing characteristics — happiness and misery, knowing and forgetting — keep alternating in me, and what does that alternation reveal about my actual position?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • When an exam result or a friend's message swings you from elation to despair within an hour, name that volatility as the jīva's, and refuse to call it your real self.
    • On social media, notice how quickly you shift between feeling admired and feeling invisible — both are happening to the conditioned passenger, not to the Lord seated within.
    • Before a high-pressure choice — a course, a relationship, a public post — ask Kṛṣṇa within as a distinct Person, not as your own inner confidence dressed up.
    householder
    • When family life delivers great happiness and misery in the same hour, recognize the pattern as the jīva's signature and stop expecting the household to deliver the Lord's steadiness.
    • In financial and career decisions, distinguish the controlled chatter of hopes and fears from the steady consultation of the controller seated in your heart.
    • Raise your children with the language of two residents — that the soul is not the body and that Kṛṣṇa Himself accompanies them — so they inherit the distinction early.
    senior
    • As the body declines, observe the misery it transmits and the happiness it occasionally offers, and verify in your own experience that you, the jīva, are the one being moved — not the Lord within.
    • Loosen attachments by daily acknowledging the eternally liberated Person seated with you, whose freedom does not depend on the body you are about to leave.
    • When mentoring juniors, transmit this verse's two-resident framing rather than generic spirituality, so they do not later confuse the controlled with the controller.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Collapsing the two residents into one, concluding that the jīva itself is the Supreme — the impersonalist misreading the verse explicitly refuses.
    • Using the Lord's presence within as license to ignore the jīva's bondage, as if simply 'remembering the Self' were already liberation.
    • Reducing the controller-controlled distinction to a psychological metaphor instead of an actual ontological difference between two persons.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share an experience this week where happiness and misery alternated rapidly in the same body — whose characteristics were those, and how do you know?
    • If Kṛṣṇa is eternally liberated and seated in this body, why does my bondage feel so total — where do you each locate the honest answer?
    • Where does the line between controller and controlled get blurred in your sādhana, and what is at stake in keeping that line sharp?
    Role Play

    One student plays a sincere seeker who insists "I am the Supreme; the Lord within is just my higher self." The other plays a Gauḍīya elder who must, using only this verse, draw out the difference between the two residents of the body without lecturing — by asking about the seeker's actual experience of happiness, misery, and control.

    Objections & Responses
    • Modern psychology already accounts for inner conflict — why import two metaphysical persons into one body when one divided mind explains it?
      A divided mind explains the conflict but not the asymmetry: one party is eternally unaffected, the other is tossed by every gain and loss. That asymmetry is not a psychological feature; it is the difference between the controller and the controlled, and only a personalist account preserves it.
    • If the Supreme Person is already within me, surrender seems redundant — I am already in His company.
      Proximity is not relationship. The verse says both reside in the same body, yet their characteristics are opposite; the conditioned soul's misery proves that mere co-location with Kṛṣṇa is not surrender. Surrender is the conscious turning of the controlled toward the controller.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.6

    ↗
    సుపర్ణావేతౌ సద‍ృశౌ సఖాయౌ యద‍ృచ్ఛయైతౌ కృతనీడౌ చ వృక్షే । ఏకస్తయో: ఖాదతి పిప్పలాన్న- మన్యో నిరన్నోఽపి బలేన భూయాన్ ॥ ౬ ॥
    suparṇāv etau sadṛśau sakhāyau
    [suparnaav etau sadrishau sakhaayau]
    yadṛcchayaitau kṛta-nīḍau ca vṛkṣe
    [yadrichchayaitau krita-needau cha vrikshe]
    ekas tayoḥ khādati pippalānnam
    [ekas tayoh khaadati pippalaannam]
    anyo niranno ’pi balena bhūyān
    [anyo niranno ’pi balena bhooyaan]

    bhūyān
    He is superior.
    By chance, two birds have made a nest together in the same tree. The two birds are friends and are of a similar nature. One of them, however, is eating the fruits of the tree, whereas the other, who does not eat the fruits, is in a superior position due to His potency.
    Purport

    The example of two birds in the same tree is given to illustrate the presence within the heart of the material body of both the individual soul and the Supersoul, the Personality of Godhead. Just as a bird makes a nest in a tree, the living entity sits within the heart. The example is appropriate because the bird is always distinct from the tree. Similarly, both the individual soul and the Supersoul are distinct entities, separate from the temporary material body. The word balena indicates that the Supreme Personality of Godhead is satisfied by His own internal potency, which consists of eternality, omniscience and bliss. As indicated by the word bhūyān, or “having superior existence,” the Supreme Lord is always in a superior position, whereas the living entity is sometimes in illusion and sometimes enlightened. The word balena indicates that the Lord is never in darkness or ignorance, but is always full in His perfect, blissful consciousness.

    Thus, the Lord is niranna, or uninterested in the bitter fruits of material activities, whereas the ordinary conditioned soul busily consumes such bitter fruits, thinking them to be sweet. Ultimately, the fruit of all material endeavor is death, but the living entity foolishly thinks material things will bring him pleasure. The word sakhāyau, or “two friends,” is also significant. Our real friend is Lord Kṛṣṇa, who is situated within our heart. Only He knows our actual needs, and only He can give us real happiness.

    Lord Kṛṣṇa is so kind that He patiently sits in the heart, trying to guide the conditioned soul back home, back to Godhead. Certainly no material friend would remain with his foolish companion for millions of years, especially if his companion were to ignore him or even curse him. But Lord Kṛṣṇa is such a faithful, loving friend that He accompanies even the most demoniac living entity and is also in the heart of the insect, pig and dog. That is because Lord Kṛṣṇa is supremely Kṛṣṇa conscious and sees every living entity as part and parcel of Himself. Every living being should give up the bitter fruits of the tree of material existence. One should turn one’s face to the Lord within the heart and revive one’s eternal loving relationship with one’s real friend, Lord Kṛṣṇa. The word sadṛśau, or “of similar nature,” indicates that both the living entity and the Personality of Godhead are conscious entities. As part and parcel of the Lord we share the Lord’s nature, but in infinitesimal quantity. Thus the Lord and the living entity are sadṛśau. A similar statement is found in the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad (4.6):

    dvā suparṇā sayujā sakhāyā samānaṁ vṛkṣaṁ pariṣasvajāte tayor anyaḥ pippalaṁ svādu atty anaśnann anyo ’bhicākaśīti

    “There are two birds in one tree. One of them is eating the fruits of the tree, while the other is witnessing the actions. The witness is the Lord, and the fruit-eater is the living entity.”

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    By chance, two birds of similar nature, who are friends, have made a nest in the same tree. One of them, however, is eating the fruits of the tree, whereas the other, superior in strength, does not eat the fruits. As birds are different from the tree, the jīva and Paramātmā are different from the body. They are similar because both are conscious entities. They are friends because they work together. There is no reason for them being together in the tree since one is attached to making a nest and eating the fruit, while the other is not attached to making a nest or eating the fruit. Vṛkṣa or tree means “that which is cut (vṛśyate) by māyā.” It refers to the body, which is destroyed by māyā. Śruti says ūrdhva-mūlam avāk-śākhaḥ vṛkṣaḥ yo veda samprati: one who knows this tree with its roots upward and branches downward becomes liberated. (Katha Upaniṣad 2.3.1) Smṛti also says ūrdhva-mūlam adhaḥ-śākham aśvatthaṁ prāhur avyayam: they say this world is an indestructible pippala tree with root upward and branches downward. (BG 15.1) In this tree (body), a nest or house (the heart) has been made out of attachment and detachment. Of the two, the jīva enjoys the fruits of karma produced by the body. Paramātmā however does not eat, but is satisfied in his own bliss by the strength of his jṣāna-śakti. Śruti says: dvā suparṇā sayujā sakhāyā samānaṁ vṛkṣaṁ pariṣasvajāte tayor anyaḥ pippalaṁ svādv atty anaśnann anyo ’bhicākaśīti Two birds who are friends embrace each other in the same tree. One bird eats the sweet fruit and the other bird remains without eating. Mundaka Upaniṣad 3.1.1
    Personal Guidance
    Two birds share the heart-tree: the jīva chews bitter fruits of karma while Kṛṣṇa, His niranna friend, waits to be turned toward.
    Practical Applications
    • Before each meal today, pause and remember that Kṛṣṇa sits in your heart as the second bird, watching with patient affection.
    • Name one "sweet" fruit you have been chewing — a grudge, a craving, a fantasy — and refuse it for a full day as an offering to the witnessing Lord.
    • Speak aloud to Kṛṣṇa within at a fixed time today, treating Him as the real sakhā who has stayed with you across every body and birth.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which bitter fruit of material life am I still mistaking for sweet, and what has it actually cost the soul that is eating it?
    • If Kṛṣṇa has sat silently in my heart through every mistake and curse, what does it mean that I have not yet turned my face toward Him?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before opening social media tonight, remember Kṛṣṇa is the second bird watching what you scroll, and choose what you would not mind Him seeing with you.
    • Pick one peer-driven craving — a brand, a body image, a relationship status — and treat it as a bitter fruit you will not bite for the next week.
    • When a friend group pressures you into something hollow, silently turn to the friend within your heart first and ask what He would have you say.
    householder
    • In the next big family or money decision, pause and consult the Lord in the heart before you consult your spreadsheet or your anxieties.
    • Name one career or possession you have been chewing as "sweet" and quietly reduce its grip this month — fewer hours, less spending, less mental rehearsal.
    • Show your children, by your own behavior, that there is a second bird in the heart — speak to Kṛṣṇa aloud at the family altar so they see Him treated as a person.
    senior
    • Each morning, sit and remember that the friend in your heart has been with you through every body you have already "transitioned through" — and will be with you through this one's end.
    • Identify one attachment — a property, a grievance, a regret — that you are still chewing, and consciously offer it to the niranna witness rather than carry it further.
    • When you mentor a younger person, point them not to your achievements but to the second bird; let your legacy be that you introduced them to their real sakhā.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading the two birds as two aspects of one impersonal Self, erasing the personalist distinction between the jīva and Kṛṣṇa.
    • Treating the Supersoul as a passive witness only, forgetting that Kṛṣṇa is the active, loving friend who is patiently guiding you home.
    • Using "both birds are sadṛśau" to claim equality with the Lord, ignoring that He alone is bhūyān — superior by His own internal potency.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one "sweet" fruit you keep returning to even though you know it is bitter — and what keeps you eating it instead of turning toward the second bird.
    • Do you actually experience Kṛṣṇa in the heart as a person and friend, or mostly as an idea? Be honest with each other about the gap.
    • If the Lord is equally seated in the heart of the insect, the pig, and the demoniac person, what does that demand of how you treat the next difficult person you meet today?
    Role Play

    One student plays a friend in crisis who insists that chasing a promotion, a relationship, or a possession will finally make them happy. The other student must respond as a Gauḍīya friend who can see both birds in the tree — without lecturing, without flattening real material concern, but steadily redirecting attention to the niranna witness within.

    Objections & Responses
    • This is just a poetic image for the conscious mind observing itself — there is no literal second bird or Supersoul in my heart.
      Kṛṣṇa is not a metaphor for self-awareness. The verse calls the two birds sakhāyau — friends, distinct persons of similar conscious nature. One is fruit-eater, the other niranna and bhūyān by His own potency. Collapsing them into one consciousness erases exactly the personal relationship the verse is pointing you toward.
    • If the Lord has been sitting in my heart all along, why has He let me suffer and make such a mess of my life?
      He has not abandoned you; you have been turned toward the fruits. Kṛṣṇa is described here as patient — accompanying even those who ignore or curse Him for millions of years. The suffering is the bitterness of the fruit itself, not His neglect. The moment you turn your face from the branch to the friend beside you, that patience becomes guidance.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.7

    ↗
    ఆత్మానమన్యం చ స వేద విద్వా- నపిప్పలాదో న తు పిప్పలాద: । యోఽవిద్యయా యుక్ స తు నిత్యబద్ధో విద్యామయో య: స తు నిత్యముక్త: ॥ ౭ ॥
    ātmānam anyaṁ ca sa veda vidvān
    [aatmaanam anyam cha sa veda vidvaan]
    apippalādo na tu pippalādaḥ
    [apippalaado na tu pippalaadah]
    yo ’vidyayā yuk sa tu nitya-baddho
    [yo ’vidyayaa yuk sa tu nitya-baddho]
    vidyā-mayo yaḥ sa tu nitya-muktaḥ
    [vidyaa-mayo yah sa tu nitya-muktah]
    Word-for-word
    ātmānam — Himself anyam — the other ca — also saḥ — He veda — knows vidvān — being omniscient apippala-adaḥ — not eating the fruits of the tree na — not tu — but pippala-adaḥ — the one who is eating the fruits of the tree yaḥ — who avidyayā — with ignorance yuk — filled saḥ — he tu — indeed nitya — eternally baddhaḥ — conditioned vidyā-mayaḥ — full of perfect knowledge yaḥ — who saḥ — he tu — indeed nitya — eternally muktaḥ — liberated.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    ātmānam anyaṁ ca sa veda vidvān
    ātmānam
    Himself
    anyam
    the other
    ca
    also
    saḥ
    He
    veda
    knows
    vidvān
    being omniscient
    apippalādo na tu pippalādaḥ
    apippala-adaḥ
    not eating the fruits of the tree
    na
    not
    tu
    but
    pippala-adaḥ
    the one who is eating the fruits of the tree
    yo ’vidyayā yuk sa tu nitya-baddho
    yaḥ
    who
    avidyayā
    with ignorance
    yuk
    filled
    saḥ
    he
    tu
    indeed
    nitya
    eternally
    baddhaḥ
    conditioned
    vidyā-mayo yaḥ sa tu nitya-muktaḥ
    vidyā-mayaḥ
    full of perfect knowledge
    yaḥ
    who
    saḥ
    he
    tu
    indeed
    nitya
    eternally
    muktaḥ
    liberated.
    The bird who does not eat the fruits of the tree is the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who by His omniscience perfectly understands His own position and that of the conditioned living entity, represented by the eating bird. That living entity, on the other hand, does not understand himself or the Lord. He is covered by ignorance and is thus called eternally conditioned, whereas the Personality of Godhead, being full of perfect knowledge, is eternally liberated.
    Purport

    The word vidyā-maya in this verse indicates the internal potency of the Lord and not the external potency, mahā-māyā. Within the material world there is vidyā, or material science, and avidyā, or material ignorance, but in this verse vidyā means the internal spiritual knowledge by which the Personality of Godhead is fixed in omniscience. The example of two birds in a tree, which is given in many Vedic literatures, demonstrates the statement nityo nityānām: there are two categories of eternal living entities, namely the Supreme Lord and the minute jīva soul. The conditioned jīva soul, forgetting his identity as an eternal servant of the Lord, tries to enjoy the fruits of his own activities and thus comes under the spell of ignorance. This bondage of ignorance has existed since time immemorial and can be rectified only by one’s taking to the loving devotional service of the Lord, which is full of spiritual knowledge. In conditioned life the living entity is forced by the laws of nature to engage in pious and impious fruitive activities, but the liberated position of every living entity is to offer the fruits of his work to the Lord, the supreme enjoyer. It should be understood that even when the living entity is in a liberated condition, his knowledge is never equal in quantity to that of the Personality of Godhead. Even Lord Brahmā, the supreme living entity within this universe, acquires only partial knowledge of the Personality of Godhead and His potencies. In Bhagavad-gītā (4.5) , the Lord explains His superior knowledge to Arjuna:

    bahūni me vyatītāni janmāni tava cārjuna tāny ahaṁ veda sarvāṇi na tvaṁ vettha parantapa

    “The Blessed Lord said: Many, many births both you and I have passed. I can remember all of them, but you cannot, O subduer of the enemy!”

    The term baddha, or “bound,” is also understood to refer to the living entity’s eternal dependence upon the Lord, either in the conditioned or liberated state. In the kingdom of māyā the living entity is bound to the cruel laws of birth and death, whereas in the spiritual sky the living entity is fixed in a bond of love to the Lord. Liberation means freedom from the miseries of life, but never freedom from one’s loving relationship with Lord Kṛṣṇa, which is the essence of one’s eternal existence. According to Śrīla Madhvācārya, the Lord is the only eternally free living entity, and all other living entities are eternally dependent and bound to the Lord, either through blissful loving service or through the bondage of māyā. The conditioned soul should give up tasting the bitter fruits of the tree of material existence and turn to his dearmost friend, Lord Kṛṣṇa, who is sitting within his heart. There is no pleasure equal to or greater than the pleasure of pure devotional service to Lord Kṛṣṇa, and by tasting the fruit of love of Kṛṣṇa, the liberated living entity enters the ocean of happiness.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Paramātmā, who does not eat the fruit, knows himself and the jīva. The jīva does not know himself or Paramātmā. The jīva, endowed with avidyā, is eternally bound. The Lord, who is endowed with cit-śakti, is eternally liberated. Paramātmā knows himself and the jīva (anyam) and does not eat the fruits of karma. The jīva, eater of the fruits of karma, does not know himself or Paramātmā. Endowed with avidyā, the jīva is eternally bound up. Paramātmā filled with the cit-śakti, is eternally liberated. Vidyā-mayaḥ refers to the cit-śakti, not to the vidyā belonging to māyā-śakti. Dvā suparṇau bhavato brāhmaṇo’ ṁśa-bhūtas tathetaro bhoktā bhavati | anyo hi sākṣī bhavatīti | vṛkṣa-dharme tau tiṣṭhataḥ | ...yatra vidyāvidye na viḍamo vidyāvidābhyāṁ bhinnaḥ | vidyāmayo hi yaḥ sa kathaṁ visayī bhavati There are two birds. One is a portion of Brahman and enjoys the fruit and the other is the witness. They remain in the tree. … In him we do not perceive vidyā and avidyā. He is different from vidyā and avidyā. How can he who is vidyāmaya be material? Gopāla-tāpanī Upaniṣad Smṛti says chāyātapau yatra na gṛdhra-pakṣau: in the Lord there is no vidyā or avidyā, no hate or favoritism. (SB 8.5.27)
    Personal Guidance
    Two birds sit in one tree: the jīva eats the fruits and forgets, Kṛṣṇa watches in full knowledge — turn to the witnessing Friend within.
    Practical Applications
    • Pause once today before reaching for a sense-pleasure and silently acknowledge the second bird — Kṛṣṇa in the heart — watching with you.
    • Name one bitter fruit of the tree of material existence you keep biting into, and refuse it once today as an offering to the Lord.
    • Before sleep, report the day's actions to the witnessing Lord within, the apippalāda bird, instead of replaying them alone in your head.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which fruit of this material tree do I keep eating even after I have tasted its bitterness, and what keeps me reaching for it again?
    • Where in my day am I living as if I were the only bird on the branch, forgetting the omniscient Friend beside me?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before opening social media, pause for one breath and remember the second bird is watching with you — then decide whether to scroll.
    • Name one 'fruit' you are chasing on campus — a grade, a relationship, an image — and ask the Lord in your heart whether it has actually tasted sweet.
    • When you feel utterly alone in an exam week or a friendship rupture, speak inwardly to Kṛṣṇa as the companion on your branch, not to an abstract 'universe.'
    householder
    • In one work or family decision today, consciously offer the fruit to Kṛṣṇa instead of clutching it as 'my outcome for my family.'
    • Notice one repeated household quarrel that is really about eating bitter fruit again — money, status, control — and step back as one who knows the second bird sees it all.
    • At day's end, sit with your spouse or alone for two minutes and report the day's actions to the Lord within, rather than only to your own anxious mind.
    senior
    • Survey the fruits you spent decades chasing and tell one younger person honestly which were bitter, so they hear it from someone who tasted them.
    • As the body weakens, practice shifting your sense of company from 'I am increasingly alone' to 'the non-eating bird has been beside me all along.'
    • Loosen one attachment this week — a possession, a grudge, a role — and offer it explicitly to Kṛṣṇa as preparation for the final crossing.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading the two-birds image as 'I am really the witness bird' — collapsing the jīva into Kṛṣṇa instead of recognizing eternal dependence on Him.
    • Treating vidyā here as ordinary material learning or self-improvement, rather than the Lord's internal potency awakened through devotional service.
    • Imagining liberation as escape from all relationship, when the verse points to exchanging the bondage of māyā for the loving bond of service to Kṛṣṇa.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a recent 'fruit' you bit into expecting sweetness and tasted bitterness — what did the second bird seem to be doing while you ate?
    • Madhvācārya says even the liberated soul remains eternally dependent on the Lord. Where does that land as relief, and where as resistance, in your own heart?
    • Where in your life are you trying to become the witness bird yourself, instead of turning to Him? Push back on each other's answers.
    Role Play

    A college student tells a senior devotee: 'I have been meditating and I feel I am the silent witness behind my thoughts — pure awareness, untouched.' The senior devotee, holding this verse, must gently distinguish the eating bird from the non-eating bird, and redirect the student from impersonal self-identification to recognizing Kṛṣṇa as the omniscient companion within.

    Objections & Responses
    • This is just a poetic way of saying my higher self watches my lower self — classic non-dual psychology dressed in Sanskrit.
      The verse explicitly separates two birds: one apippalāda, fully knowing Himself and the other, and one pippalāda, ignorant of both. Prabhupāda cites nityo nityānām — two categories of eternal beings, never collapsed into one. The witnessing bird is not a deeper layer of you; He is Kṛṣṇa, a distinct Person on the same branch, waiting for you to turn and serve Him.
    • If the jīva has been bound 'since time immemorial,' that sounds unjust — why should I be punished for ignorance I never chose?
      The verse does not describe punishment but a misuse of independence: the jīva turned from servant to enjoyer and so came under avidyā. The same verse names the remedy — vidyā, the Lord's internal potency, awakened through devotional service. The question is not 'why am I bound?' but 'will I, today, turn to the Friend on the branch beside me?'
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.8

    ↗
    దేహస్థోఽపి న దేహస్థో విద్వాన్ స్వప్నాద్ యథోత్థిత: । అదేహస్థోఽపి దేహస్థ: కుమతి: స్వప్నద‍ృగ్ యథా ॥ ౮ ॥
    deha-stho ’pi na deha-stho
    [deha-stho ’pi na deha-stho]
    vidvān svapnād yathotthitaḥ
    [vidvaan svapnaad yathotthitah]
    adeha-stho ’pi deha-sthaḥ
    [adeha-stho ’pi deha-sthah]
    kumatiḥ svapna-dṛg yathā
    [kumatih svapna-drig yathaa]
    Word-for-word
    deha — in the material body sthaḥ — situated api — although na — not deha — in the body sthaḥ — situated vidvān — an enlightened person svapnāt — from a dream yathā — just as utthitaḥ — having risen adeha — not in the body sthaḥ — situated api — although deha — in the body sthaḥ — situated ku-matiḥ — a foolish person svapna — a dream dṛk — seeing yathā — just as.
