    One who is enlightened in self-realization, although living within the material body, sees himself as transcendental to the body, just as one who has arisen from a dream gives up identification with the dream body. A foolish person, however, although not identical with his material body but transcendental to it, thinks himself to be situated in the body, just as one who is dreaming sees himself as situated in an imaginary body.
    Purport

    In Lord Kṛṣṇa’s discussion of the different characteristics of liberated and conditioned souls, the Lord first clarified the distinction between the eternally liberated Personality of Godhead and the marginal potency, the innumerable jīvas, who are sometimes conditioned and sometimes liberated. In this and the next nine verses, the Lord describes the different symptoms of liberated and conditioned jīva souls. In a dream one sees oneself in an imaginary body, but upon waking one gives up all identification with that body. Similarly, one who has awakened to Kṛṣṇa consciousness no longer identifies with the gross or subtle material bodies, nor does he become affected by the happiness and distress of material life. On the other hand, a foolish person ( kumati ) does not awaken from the dream of material existence and is afflicted with innumerable problems due to false identification with the gross and subtle material bodies. One should become situated in one’s eternal spiritual identity ( nitya-svarūpa ). By properly identifying oneself as the eternal servant of Kṛṣṇa, one becomes relieved of his false material identity, and therefore the miseries of illusory existence immediately cease, just as the anxiety of a troublesome dream ceases as soon as one awakens to his normal, pleasant surroundings. It should be understood, however, that the analogy of awakening from a dream can never be applied to the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who is never in illusion. The Lord is eternally awake and enlightened in His own unique category called viṣṇu-tattva. Such knowledge is easily understood by one who is vidvān, or enlightened in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The liberated jīva, though living in the body, is not situated there, just as person on waking from a dream is not situated in the dream body. The bound jīva however, though not his body, identifies with it like a person who identifies with his body in a dream. The difference between the bound and liberated jīva is now explained in ten verses. Three verses answer the questions “How does the liberated person exist in the body?” The liberated person (vidvān) is not situated in the body under the control of previous impressions. Awaking from a dream, though situated in the dream by remembrance, the person is not situated there. This is similar to the supposed condition of bādhitānuvṛtti. [Note: The liberated soul continues to have an appearance of dualistic vision after the illusion has been destroyed, but this does not create bondage. This is called bādhitānuvṛtti. This is proposed by followers of Śaṅkara.] In that condition, he is not moved by the happiness and distress in the dream, since he is fixed in waking consciousness. Therefore he is not actually situated in the body. The bound jīva however is situated in his body and experiences happiness and distress. This is like an ignorant person who, seeing a dream, participates in the dream with his dream body.
    Personal Guidance
    The vidvān lives in the body without being the body — wake from the dream of material identification and stand in your nitya-svarūpa as Kṛṣṇa's servant.
    Practical Applications
    • Each time you catch yourself saying "I am tired," "I am insulted," "I am fat," silently correct it to "this body is" — and remember the dreamer who has just woken.
    • Begin the day by formally identifying as the eternal servant of Kṛṣṇa before checking the mirror, the phone, or any other report on the body.
    • When a wave of bodily happiness or distress hits today, refuse to act on it for sixty seconds and observe it the way you would observe a fading dream upon waking.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which specific bodily identification — age, looks, health, gender, race, designation — am I still treating as the bedrock of "me," and what would change in my day if I saw it as dream-body?
    • Where in my life am I behaving like the kumati — fully awake to the world's verdict on my body, but still asleep to my identity as Kṛṣṇa's eternal servant?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before posting or scrolling, pause and notice that the "self" being curated in the feed is a dream-body — then ask whether you would still post the same thing standing as Kṛṣṇa's servant.
    • When an exam result, a breakup, or a friend group's verdict tries to define who you are this week, repeat to yourself that this is the dream body's news, not yours, and act from that footing.
    • Pick one bodily comparison you make daily — height, skin, weight, attractiveness, performance — and for one week refuse to let it dictate a single decision, treating it explicitly as kumati-vision.
    householder
    • When tension with spouse, child, or boss escalates because of bodily fatigue, hunger, or hormones, name it as such before reacting, and act from the vidvān's footing rather than the body's.
    • In financial and career decisions this month, distinguish what serves the family's real welfare as souls from what merely soothes bodily anxiety about status, comfort, or future security.
    • Teach your children, by example more than words, that Mom and Dad are not their bodies — let them see you uphold sādhana on a sick day, a tired day, and a day the body is celebrated.
    senior
    • Each time the body announces a new limitation — a joint, a memory lapse, a diagnosis — treat it as the dream thinning out, not as the death of the self, and tell at least one younger person what you are seeing.
    • Loosen one specific attachment this month — a possession, a role, a grievance — that you are clinging to mainly because the dream body has held it for so long.
    • Use whatever time and energy remains to be a vidvān in the eyes of grandchildren and juniors: not lecturing them on the soul, but visibly unbewildered by what bewilders everyone else.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Using "I am not this body" to neglect health, family duties, or basic decency — the verse describes the vidvān's vision, not a license for irresponsibility.
    • Mistaking a philosophical opinion ("I agree I am the soul") for the actual waking the verse describes, which only comes through sustained sādhana and identification as Kṛṣṇa's servant.
    • Drifting into impersonal language — "the Self," "pure awareness" — and quietly dropping the personalist anchor that the awakened one is the servant of Kṛṣṇa, not a body-free abstraction.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a recent moment where bodily happiness or distress completely defined your reality for an hour or a day — what would it have looked like to treat that experience as a dream you had woken from?
    • Where do members of this group draw the line between healthy care of the body and false identification with it — and where do you actually disagree?
    • If "awakening" here is not a one-time event but a stable vision, what concrete sādhana in your life is actually producing that vision, and what is producing the opposite?
    Role Play

    One student plays a person who has just received a difficult medical diagnosis and is convinced "this is happening to me." Another plays a Gauḍīya friend who must — without spiritual bypassing, without denying the body's care, and without impersonal language — help them recover the vision of themselves as a soul awakening from a dream body and as Kṛṣṇa's eternal servant. A third observes and afterward names where the helper slipped into platitude, callousness, or impersonalism.

    Objections & Responses
    • This sounds like dissociation dressed up as spirituality — telling people in real bodily suffering that their body is just a dream is dangerous.
      The verse does not deny that the body is experienced; it denies that the body is the self. The vidvān still feeds, rests, and treats the body — but he is not crushed by it, because he knows the sufferer of bodily pain is not who he ultimately is. That is the opposite of dissociation: it is correct identification, which actually enables steady, compassionate care of the body without being enslaved by it.
    • Why specifically Kṛṣṇa? Plenty of traditions teach "you are not the body" without the personalist framing.
      Prabhupāda is precise here: the awakening described is not into an impersonal Self but into nitya-svarūpa as the eternal servant of Kṛṣṇa. Without that personal anchor, "I am not the body" tends to collapse into a new, subtler ego — "I am the Absolute" — which is just the dream rearranged. The vidvān wakes into a relationship, not into a void.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.9

    ↗
    ఇన్ద్రియైరిన్ద్రియార్థేషు గుణైరపి గుణేషు చ । గృహ్యమాణేష్వహం కుర్యాన్న విద్వాన్ యస్త్వవిక్రియ: ॥ ౯ ॥
    indriyair indriyārtheṣu
    [indriyair indriyaartheshu]
    guṇair api guṇeṣu ca
    [gunair api guneshu cha]
    gṛhyamāṇeṣv ahaṁ kuryān
    [grihyamaaneshv aham kuryaan]
    na vidvān yas tv avikriyaḥ
    [na vidvaan yas tv avikriyah]
    Word-for-word
    indriyaiḥ — by the senses indriya — of the senses artheṣu — in the objects guṇaiḥ — by those generated from the modes of nature api — even guṇeṣu — in those generated by the same modes ca — also gṛhyamāṇeṣu — as they are being accepted aham — false ego kuryāt — should create na — not vidvān — one who is enlightened yaḥ — who tu — indeed avikriyaḥ — is not affected by material desire.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    is not affected by material desire.
    An enlightened person who is free from the contamination of material desire does not consider himself to be the performer of bodily activities; rather, he knows that in all such activities it is only the senses, born of the modes of nature, that are contacting sense objects born of the same modes of nature.
    Purport

    Lord Kṛṣṇa makes a similar statement in Bhagavad-gītā (3.28) :

    tattva-vit tu mahā-bāho guṇa-karma-vibhāgayoḥ guṇā guṇeṣu vartanta iti matvā na sajjate

    “One who is in knowledge of the Absolute Truth, O mighty-armed, does not engage himself in the senses and sense gratification, knowing well the difference between work in devotion and work for fruitive results.”

    The material body always interacts with the sense objects, for in order to survive the body must eat, drink, speak, sleep, and so on, but an enlightened person who knows the science of Kṛṣṇa consciousness never thinks, “I am accepting these sense objects as my property. They are meant for my pleasure.” Similarly, if the body performs a wonderful activity, a Kṛṣṇa conscious person does not become proud, nor is he depressed by the failure of the body to function in a particular way. In other words, Kṛṣṇa consciousness means giving up identification with the gross and subtle material bodies. One should see them as the external energy of the Lord, working under the direction of the Lord’s empowered representative māyā. One absorbed in fruitive activities works under the jurisdiction of mahā-māyā, or the external illusory potency, and experiences the miseries of material existence. On the other hand, a devotee works under the internal potency, called yoga-māyā, and remains satisfied by offering his loving service to the Lord. In either case, the Lord Himself, by His multifarious potencies, is the ultimate performer of action.

    According to Śrīla Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura, one who claims to be transcendental to the bodily concept of life, but at the same time remains under the influence of material desire and mental transformation, is understood to be a cheater and the lowest type of conditioned soul.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    When the sense objects are received by the senses, the liberated person, who has no transformations arising from ahaṅkāra, does not think “I am experiencing this,” since it is only material guṇas received by other material guṇas. When the sense objects are received by the senses, the liberated person does not think “I am experiencing this,” since it is only material guṇas received by other material guṇas. The sign of the person without ahaṅkāra is that he is devoid of transformations arising from ahaṅkāra. But a person who undergoes transformations and still says “I am doing nothing at all” is a fraud, deeply bound up in māyā.
    Personal Guidance
    The enlightened soul sees the senses, born of the guṇas, interacting with sense objects also born of the guṇas — he is not the doer.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice one moment today when the body eats, speaks, or scrolls, and quietly recognize: "the guṇas are moving among the guṇas — I am the witness."
    • When a task succeeds or fails today, refuse both pride and depression — offer the action and its outcome to Kṛṣṇa as the real performer.
    • Speak about your work today without using "I did," "I achieved," or "I failed" — practice locating yourself as the soul, not the body.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where am I still claiming bodily activities — eating, speaking, working — as "my" doing, and what does that claim cost me?
    • Viśvanātha Cakravartī warns of the "cheater" who speaks of transcendence while clinging to desire — where in my own life does that pattern hide?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • After your next exam result or social media post, catch the spike of "I crushed it" or "I'm a failure" and replace it with: "the senses and guṇas met their objects — Kṛṣṇa is the doer."
    • Pick one peer-pressure moment this week — a party, a comparison, a trend — and notice the guṇas pulling the senses toward objects, then choose your response from the soul, not the body.
    • Refuse to build your identity on a single grade, follower count, or rejection; rehearse seeing them as guṇas interacting with guṇas, while you remain the unchanged witness.
    householder
    • Before a big family decision — a job change, a school for the kids, a purchase — pause and ask whether "I" am deciding or the modes of nature are pushing the senses; then offer the choice to Kṛṣṇa.
    • When your spouse or child praises or blames you today, receive it without inflating or collapsing — the body acted, the guṇas moved, and you can return to your sva-dharma steadily.
    • Track one full workday with the inner note "the senses are contacting sense objects under Kṛṣṇa's potency," and see how it changes your tone at dinner with the family.
    senior
    • As the body ages and capacities shift, practice naming each loss as "the guṇas withdrawing from the guṇas" — not the diminishment of you, the soul.
    • When you mentor a younger person, refuse to claim authorship of their progress; show them by example that the vidvān credits Kṛṣṇa and the guṇas, not himself.
    • Loosen one long-held attachment this month — a possession, a role, a grievance — by recognizing it as the body's history, not the soul's identity, and gently offer it to Kṛṣṇa.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Using "the guṇas are acting, not me" as an excuse for careless work, indulgence, or moral evasion.
    • Mistaking this verse's witnessing stance for impersonal detachment, instead of active service to Kṛṣṇa under yoga-māyā.
    • Imitating the language of the vidvān outwardly while inwardly still hungry for sense pleasure — the very cheating Viśvanātha names.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a recent situation where you took personal credit or personal blame for what was really the body and senses doing their work — what would the vidvān of this verse have done instead?
    • Prabhupāda contrasts working under mahā-māyā with working under yoga-māyā — debate with a partner: in your daily routine, which potency is actually directing most of your activity?
    • Discuss the line between healthy responsibility and false ego-identification with action — where do you and your partner draw it differently?
    Role Play

    Two students pair up. Student A has just been praised at work for a major success and feels the surge of "I did this." Student B, playing a senior Vaiṣṇava, must respond using this verse — without deflating A's joy, redirect the credit to Kṛṣṇa and the guṇas, and help A see the danger of claiming doership. Then switch: A is criticized harshly for a failure and feels crushed; B applies the same verse to lift A out of bodily identification.

    Objections & Responses
    • If the senses act on sense objects and I am not the doer, doesn't this dissolve all moral responsibility? Why not just indulge?
      The verse describes the vision of the vidvān who is already free of material desire — it is the fruit of purification, not a license preceding it. Viśvanātha Cakravartī explicitly calls one who claims this status while still ruled by desire a cheater. Responsibility deepens, not dissolves: you stop claiming credit, but you also stop directing the senses toward your own gratification and instead engage them in Kṛṣṇa's service.
    • This sounds like the impersonal Advaita position — the Self is mere witness, action is just nature unfolding. How is this different?
      Prabhupāda's purport makes the distinction sharp: the activities are not unfolding in an impersonal void but under Kṛṣṇa's own potencies — mahā-māyā for the conditioned, yoga-māyā for the devotee. The Lord Himself, by His multifarious potencies, is the ultimate performer. The witnessing here is not retreat into a featureless Self but recognition of Kṛṣṇa as the actual agent, freeing the devotee to serve Him personally.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.10

    ↗
    దైవాధీనే శరీరేఽస్మిన్ గుణభావ్యేన కర్మణా । వర్తమానోఽబుధస్తత్ర కర్తాస్మీతి నిబధ్యతే ॥ ౧౦ ॥
    daivādhīne śarīre ’smin
    [daivaadheene shareere ’smin]
    guṇa-bhāvyena karmaṇā
    [guna-bhaavyena karmanaa]
    vartamāno ’budhas tatra
    [vartamaano ’budhas tatra]
    kartāsmīti nibadhyate
    [kartaasmeeti nibadhyate]
    Word-for-word
    daiva — of one’s previous fruitive activities adhīne — which is under the influence śarīre — in the material body asmin — in this guṇa — by the modes of nature bhāvyena — which are produced karmaṇā — by fruitive activities vartamānaḥ — being situated abudhaḥ — one who is foolish tatra — within the bodily functions kartā — the doer asmi — I am iti — thus nibadhyate — is bound up.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    daivādhīne śarīre ’smin
    daiva

    adhīne
    which is under the influence
    śarīre

    guṇa-bhāvyena karmaṇā

    kartāsmīti nibadhyate

    being situated

    tatra

    is bound up.
    An unintelligent person situated within the body created by his previous fruitive activities thinks, “I am the performer of action.” Bewildered by false ego, such a foolish person is therefore bound up by fruitive activities, which are in fact carried out by the modes of nature.
    Purport

    As stated in Bhagavad-gītā (3.27) :

    prakṛteḥ kriyamāṇāni guṇaiḥ karmāṇi sarvaśaḥ ahaṅkāra-vimūḍhātmā kartāham iti manyate

    The living entity is dependent on the supreme entity, Lord Kṛṣṇa, but because of false pride he ignores the Supreme Personality of Godhead and considers himself to be the performer of action and enjoyer of everything. Śrīla Madhvācārya states that just as a king punishes a rebellious subject, the Supreme Lord punishes the sinful living entity by forcing him to transmigrate from one body to another in the network of the illusory energy.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The bound jīva, situated in his body produced by previous karma, thinking, “I am the doer” is bound to the body by actions arising from his senses. The fool situated in his body which is dependent on previous karmas is bound by actions produced by the senses (guṇa-bhāvyena). Why? By ahaṅkāra he thinks, “I am the doer.” It is said: prakṛteḥ kriyamāṇāni guṇaiḥ karmāṇi sarvaśaḥ | ahaṅkāra-vimūḍhātmā kartāham iti manyate || The person bewildered by pride thinks that he is the doer of actions which are being done completely by the senses made of prakṛti. BG 3.27
    Personal Guidance
    The body is shaped by past karma and run by the guṇas; the bewildered soul, posing as doer, binds itself further — step out of that false ahaṅkāra.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice one moment today when you silently take credit ("I did this") and inwardly reassign the doership to Kṛṣṇa and the guṇas working through your conditioning.
    • Before any task, pause five seconds and offer it: "Kṛṣṇa, this body is Yours, this work is Yours" — then act with full effort but without the kartā-asmi posture.
    • Refuse the modern script of self-made success — write down one recent "win" and list the inherited body, intelligence, circumstances, and helpers that actually carried it.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my life am I most invested in the identity of being "the one who did it," and what fear is hiding inside that claim?
    • If this body and its tendencies are a karmic loan running on the guṇas, what does honest, non-bewildered action look like for me today?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • After your next exam or post, catch the inner voice running the score — "I crushed it / I failed" — and rename it: "this body and these guṇas performed; let me see what Kṛṣṇa wants me to do next."
    • Pick one area where you compare yourself relentlessly with peers (grades, looks, followers) and for one week journal each comparison as evidence of kartā-asmi delusion rather than identity-shaping truth.
    • Before a high-stakes effort — interview, audition, confession to a crush — offer it aloud to Kṛṣṇa, do your absolute best, and refuse to let the result rewrite who you think you are.
    householder
    • When you catch yourself thinking "I built this family / I earn this income / I raised these kids," pause and itemize the body, partner, parents, teachers, employers, and circumstances Kṛṣṇa arranged — then continue the work as caretaker, not proprietor.
    • In your next disagreement with spouse or child about a decision, drop the "I know best because I did X" card for one full conversation and listen as someone whose intelligence is also guṇa-conditioned.
    • Set up a weekly money-and-work review where you formally offer the week's earnings and efforts to Kṛṣṇa before planning the next — let provision feel like prasāda, not personal conquest.
    senior
    • Walk through your life's major accomplishments and re-narrate each one to a younger relative without the word "I" as subject — notice how much of the story still holds, and how much was ahaṅkāra.
    • Identify one attachment to legacy — a building, a reputation, a family business — and consciously loosen your grip this month by handing over real decision-making to a successor.
    • As the body visibly declines, make a daily practice of saying "this body was given, is going, and was never the doer; the soul is Kṛṣṇa's servant" — and let that line replace the lifelong reflex of self-credit and self-blame.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Using "the guṇas are doing it" as a fatalistic excuse to slack off, skip duties, or evade accountability for harmful choices.
    • Trading gross ahaṅkāra for a subtler spiritual ahaṅkāra — "I am the surrendered devotee, I am the non-doer" — which is the same false ego in saffron clothes.
    • Hearing this verse as impersonal detachment from action rather than as a call to act under Kṛṣṇa's shelter, free of the kartā-asmi delusion.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a recent achievement you are proud of — then dissect with your partner how much was "you" versus body, guṇas, upbringing, and grace; where do you disagree about the line?
    • If the verse says the foolish person is bound precisely by claiming doership, why does ordinary life — schools, workplaces, families — reward exactly that claim? How do we live inside that tension?
    • Discuss a time you blamed someone harshly for their actions; how would your response shift if you took seriously that they were "situated within a body created by previous fruitive activities"?
    Role Play

    One student plays a high-performing professional who has just been passed over for a promotion and is venting — "I did everything, I earned this, they robbed me." The other plays a Gauḍīya friend who must, without preaching or platitudes, gently expose the kartā-asmi-nibadhyate pattern in the venting and offer a doable next step rooted in this verse.

    Objections & Responses
    • This sounds like a recipe for passivity and learned helplessness — if the guṇas are doing everything, why try, why reform anything, why hold anyone responsible?
      The verse does not deny action; it denies the false proprietorship of action. Kṛṣṇa repeatedly commands vigorous, disciplined work — Arjuna is told to fight, not to sit. What is dismantled is the ahaṅkāra that claims the work as mine and the fruits as my due. A devotee works harder than the materialist, because the work is service, not self-construction; and responsibility actually deepens, because we answer to Kṛṣṇa, not merely to our own ego.
    • Modern psychology says a strong sense of agency and self-authorship is essential to mental health; your verse seems to undermine the very ego people need to function.
      Gauḍīya teaching distinguishes ahaṅkāra (the false identification "I am this body, I am the doer") from a healthy functional self — the eternal servant of Kṛṣṇa, who acts, decides, and grows. We are not asked to dissolve into nothing; we are asked to relocate our sense of self from the karma-built body to the soul in relationship with Kṛṣṇa. That relocation, far from collapsing function, produces the steadiest agency human beings can know, because it no longer depends on outcomes we cannot control.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.11

    ↗
    ఏవం విరక్త: శయన ఆసనాటనమజ్జనే । దర్శనస్పర్శనఘ్రాణభోజనశ్రవణాదిషు । న తథా బధ్యతే విద్వాన్ తత్ర తత్రాదయన్ గుణాన్ ॥ ౧౧ ॥
    evaṁ viraktaḥ śayana
    [evam viraktah shayana]

    An enlightened person fixed in detachment engages his body in lying down, sitting, walking, bathing, seeing, touching, smelling, eating, hearing and so on, but is never entangled by such activities. Indeed, remaining as a witness to all bodily functions, he merely engages his bodily senses with their objects and does not become entangled like an unintelligent person.
    Purport

    In the previous chapter, Uddhava asked Lord Kṛṣṇa why an enlightened person, just like a conditioned soul, engages in external bodily functions. Here is the Lord’s answer. While engaged in bodily functions, an unintelligent person is attached to both the means and end of material life and therefore experiences intense lamentation and jubilation on the material platform. A self-realized soul, however, studies the inevitable defeat and suffering of ordinary persons and does not make the mistake of trying to enjoy the bodily functions even slightly. He instead remains a detached witness, merely engaging his senses in the normal functions of bodily maintenance. As indicated here by the word ādayan, he engages something other than his actual self in material experience.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    An enlightened person fixed in detachment engages his body in lying down, sitting, walking, bathing, seeing, touching, smelling, eating, hearing and so on, but is never bound by such activities. Situated as a witness to all these actions, he merely engages his bodily senses with their sense objects. Uddhava asked “How does he enjoy, how does he sleep and eat?” (SB 11.10.36) This is answered in three verses. The bound jīva is bound up by joy and sorrow arising from his actions for attaining sleep, sitting or eating, out of attachment. But the liberated person is not bound while doing these activities even though he may experience a little joy or sorrow as in bādhitānuvṛtti. This is because he is detached. Though he engages his senses in sense objects he remains as the witness and does not personally involve himself.
    Personal Guidance
    The vidvān engages body and senses in their natural functions while remaining a detached witness — sense-objects pass through, but never entangle him.
    Practical Applications
    • Pick one routine act today — eating, bathing, or walking — and perform it as bodily maintenance offered to Kṛṣṇa, not as private enjoyment.
    • When a sense-pleasure arises (a taste, a sight, a touch), notice it as something happening to the body and refuse the inner claim 'I am enjoying this.'
    • Stop labeling neutral bodily functions as 'my treats' or 'my deserved rest' — engage them with the sober economy of one maintaining a borrowed instrument.
    Reflection Questions
    • In which ordinary activity — eating, scrolling, resting — do I still smuggle in the enjoyer-mood that this verse asks me to drop?
    • What would it concretely change in my day if I treated my senses as workers engaging their objects, rather than as channels of 'my' pleasure?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Eat one meal today in the dining hall without performing it for Instagram or narrating the taste to yourself — just feed the body and return to your work.
    • When a notification, a crush's glance, or a song hits your senses, name silently 'sense meeting object' instead of 'this is making me feel something real about me.'
    • Refuse the campus script that says authentic living means maximizing every sensory experience — try one weekend of plain sleeping, plain eating, plain study, and watch what falls away.
    householder
    • When you eat with your family tonight, engage the food as bodily maintenance and as service to those you feed, not as your private reward for a hard day.
    • Notice the moments at work or home where you tell yourself 'I have earned this comfort' — and instead engage the comfort as a tool to keep the body fit for duty and bhakti.
    • Walk through your routines — commute, chores, bath, bed — with the sober understanding that you are operating a body on Kṛṣṇa's behalf, not collecting daily pleasures owed to you.
    senior
    • As the body ages and senses dull, refuse the temptation to clutch at remaining pleasures — receive food, rest, and sight as quiet maintenance, not as last enjoyments to be hoarded.
    • When you sit, walk, or lie down with effort now, treat each act as a chance to practice the vidvān's witnessing — the body is doing what bodies do, and you are not it.
    • Tell a younger family member, without preaching, how your relationship to taste, sleep, and comfort has changed — model the calm engagement of senses that this verse describes.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Mistaking detachment for suppression — refusing to eat, sleep, or bathe properly, when the verse explicitly sanctions these bodily functions.
    • Performing the outer routines normally but secretly rehearsing the enjoyer-identity, so the 'witness' stance becomes a thin spiritual costume.
    • Reading 'witness' as impersonal awareness or the inner Self, instead of the Kṛṣṇa-conscious vidvān who knows he is not the body and serves the Lord.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one bodily activity each of you finds it hardest to perform without subtly claiming enjoyership — and why that one and not another.
    • Where in your week do you mistake renunciation for awkward avoidance of normal bodily functions, rather than this verse's calm engagement-without-entanglement?
    • Discuss whether the 'witness' described here is the same as the 'mindful observer' of modern meditation culture, or something fundamentally different — defend your answer.
    Role Play

    Two students share a meal. One eats slowly, savoring every bite, narrating how 'present' and 'mindful' they are being. The other eats simply, as bodily maintenance, and afterward returns to study. The first student challenges the second: 'You are missing the gift of the senses — Kṛṣṇa gave us taste for a reason.' Play out the conversation as the second student, defending the vidvān's stance from this verse without sliding into harshness or false renunciation.

    Objections & Responses
    • If the enlightened person does all the same activities as everyone else — eating, sleeping, walking, seeing — what real difference does this 'detachment' make? It sounds like an invisible, untestable inner claim.
      The difference is not in the outer act but in who is identified as the actor and enjoyer. The unintelligent person rides waves of lamentation and jubilation tied to those acts; the vidvān does not. Test it by the inner economy — whether loss of the sense-object collapses you. The vidvān engages the senses as a caretaker engages a horse, not as a man chasing his own pleasure.
    • Modern psychology tells us to fully feel and enjoy our embodied experience — taste, touch, presence. This verse sounds like dissociation dressed up as spirituality.
      Dissociation is rejection of experience; the vidvān does not reject — he engages 'lying down, sitting, walking, bathing, seeing, touching, smelling, eating, hearing.' What he refuses is the false claim 'I am the body enjoying this.' That refusal is not numbness but accurate self-knowledge: the soul is eternal servant of Kṛṣṇa, the body is His instrument. Real presence is knowing who is actually present.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.12-13

    ↗
    ప్రకృతిస్థోఽప్యసంసక్తో యథా ఖం సవితానిల: । వైశారద్యేక్షయాసఙ్గశితయా ఛిన్నసంశయ: ॥ ౧౨ ॥ ప్రతిబుద్ధ ఇవ స్వప్నాన్నానాత్వాద్ వినివర్తతే ॥ ౧౩ ॥
    prakṛti-stho ’py asaṁsakto
    [prakriti-stho ’py asamsakto]
    yathā khaṁ savitānilaḥ
    [yathaa kham savitaanilah]
    vaiśāradyekṣayāsaṅga-
    [vaishaaradyekshayaasanga-]
    śitayā chinna-saṁśayaḥ
    [shitayaa chinna-samshayah]
    pratibuddha iva svapnān
    [pratibuddha iva svapnaan]
    nānātvād vinivartate
    [naanaatvaad vinivartate]
    Word-for-word
    prakṛti — in the material world sthaḥ — situated api — even though asaṁsaktaḥ — completely detached from sense gratification yathā — just as kham — the sky savitā — the sun anilaḥ — the wind vaiśāradyā — by most expert īkṣayā — vision asaṅga — through detachment śitayā — sharpened chinna — cut to pieces saṁśayaḥ — doubts pratibuddhaḥ — awakened iva — like svapnāt — from a dream nānātvāt — from the duality of variety of the material world vinivartate — one turns away or renounces.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    one turns away or renounces.
    Although the sky, or space, is the resting place of everything, the sky does not mix with anything, nor is it entangled. Similarly, the sun is not at all attached to the water in which it is reflected within innumerable reservoirs, and the mighty wind blowing everywhere is not affected by the innumerable aromas and atmospheres through which it passes. In the same way, a self-realized soul is completely detached from the material body and the material world around it. He is like a person who has awakened and arisen from a dream. With expert vision sharpened by detachment, the self-realized soul cuts all doubts to pieces through knowledge of the self and completely withdraws his consciousness from the expansion of material variety.
    Purport

    According to Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura, a self-realized soul cuts all doubts to pieces by direct experience of his true spiritual identity. The Supreme Personality of Godhead is Lord Kṛṣṇa, and there is no possibility of any existence separate from Lord Kṛṣṇa. Such expert knowledge cuts all doubts to pieces. As stated here, prakṛti-stho ’py asaṁsaktaḥ: like the sky, the sun or the wind, one who is self-realized is not entangled, though situated within the material creation of the Lord. Nānātva, or “material variety,” refers to one’s material body, the bodies of others and the unlimited paraphernalia for bodily sense gratification, both physical and mental. By awakening to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, one completely retires from illusory sense gratification and becomes absorbed in the progressive realization of the soul situated within the body. As revealed in the example of the two birds in a tree, both the individual soul and the Personality of Godhead are completely separate from the gross and subtle material bodies. If one turns one’s face to the Lord, recognizing one’s eternal dependence on Him, there will be no further suffering or anxiety, even though one is still situated within the material world. The unlimited experiences of material objects only increase one’s anxiety, whereas perception of the Absolute Truth, Śrī Kṛṣṇa, immediately brings one to the platform of peace. Thus one who is intelligent retires from the world of matter and becomes a fully self-realized Kṛṣṇa conscious person.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Though situated in prakṛti, the liberated person is unattached, just as ether, the sun and air are unaffected by everything they pervade. Having cut all doubts by sharp detachment and discerning vision, he renounces all material bodies as if fully awakened from a dream. Why is he not affected? Just as ether is situated everywhere but is not attached to anything, just as the sun distributes its rays everywhere but is not affected, just as air spreads everywhere without being affected, after having destroyed all doubts such as his fitness for liberation by sharp detachment (asaṅgena), he renounces varieties of bodies (nānātvāt) in this world.
    Personal Guidance
    Like sky, sun, and wind untouched by what they pervade, the self-realized soul stays in the world yet cuts every doubt with vision sharpened by detachment.
    Practical Applications
    • Today, when a difficult situation tries to define you, silently rehearse: "I am situated in this, like sky in space, but I am not mixed with it."
    • Catch one doubt about your spiritual identity as it arises and answer it directly from Kṛṣṇa's word rather than letting it loop in your mind.
    • Treat one routine sensory pleasure as a reflection on water, not the sun itself, and withdraw the extra attention you usually give it.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where am I still mixing with the material situation I am only meant to pervade, the way the sky never mixes with what rests in it?
    • Which doubt about my eternal relationship with Kṛṣṇa have I left uncut, and what variety of material life is feeding it?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • When a friend group's mood, a grade, or a feed tries to tell you who you are, name it inwardly as nānātva — variety the wind passes through, not the wind itself.
    • Take one doubt you keep Googling about your direction in life and bring it instead to a senior devotee or to Kṛṣṇa's instructions, asking to have it cut, not soothed.
    • Pick one sense input you reach for on autopilot — short videos, late-night snacks, a particular notification — and treat it for a week as a reflection on water you can simply not chase.
    householder
    • In the next family or work conflict, locate yourself like the sky in the room — fully present, hosting everyone's emotions, refusing to get mixed up in them as if they were your own identity.
    • When financial or career anxiety spikes, write down the specific doubt about Kṛṣṇa's care for your family and answer it from scripture before you act, so decisions are made by knowledge, not fear.
    • Choose one area — phone use, food, entertainment — where the household has drifted into "unlimited experiences of material objects," and quietly contract it to what actually supports sādhana and service.
    senior
    • As the body changes, practice the verse literally: each morning, identify yourself as the one situated within this body, like sky in a room, watching it age without becoming it.
    • Take stock of one lingering doubt or regret from earlier life and let it be cut now by knowledge of the self, rather than carried into the next chapter as unfinished material variety.
    • When mentoring children, grandchildren, or younger devotees, speak from the awakened-from-dream perspective — naming material entanglements gently, by their real name, so they hear it from someone who has walked through them.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Mistaking emotional numbness or indifference for the asaṅga vision the verse describes, when real detachment comes from sharpened knowledge of the self.
    • Using "I am not this body" as a slogan to dodge responsibilities, while still being fully entangled in the mind's preferences and aversions.
    • Treating "awakening from the dream" as a one-time event, instead of a daily withdrawal from nānātva back to Kṛṣṇa consciousness.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Sky, sun, water-reflection, wind: which of these images best exposes how you are currently entangled, and why that one?
    • Share a recent doubt about your spiritual identity — did knowledge cut it, or did you simply distract yourself until it faded?
    • Where do we draw the line between healthy engagement with family and work, and the "expansion of material variety" the verse asks us to withdraw from?
    Role Play

    One student plays a self-realized practitioner who has just lost a major material stake — a job, a property, a reputation. Another plays a sympathetic friend insisting they should be devastated. The practitioner must respond from the verse: situated in the loss, but unmixed with it, using the sky, sun, and wind images to explain — not as performance, but as honest report. The group then critiques where it sounded like real asaṅga and where it sounded like denial.

    Objections & Responses
    • This sounds like spiritual bypassing — pretending nothing affects you while life is actually painful and complicated.
      The verse does not ask you to suppress feeling; it asks you to see clearly. The sky still holds the storm, the sun still touches the water — engagement is real. What changes is the false identification: you no longer think the storm is happening to you. That is sharpened vision, not numbness, and it is tested by how cleanly you keep serving Kṛṣṇa and others while inside the difficulty.
    • If a self-realized soul is "completely separate" from the body, why bother with diet, duty, family, or service at all?
      Because the body and its duties are Kṛṣṇa's arrangement, not yours to discard on a whim. The verse compares the soul to sky, sun, and wind — none of which abandon their field of activity; they simply remain unentangled within it. Real detachment looks like doing your prescribed duty more carefully, not less, because you are no longer using it as a stage for ego.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.14

    ↗
    యస్య స్యుర్వీతసఙ్కల్పా: ప్రాణేన్ద్రియమనోధియామ్ । వృత్తయ: స వినిర్ముక్తో దేహస్థోఽపి హి తద్గుణై: ॥ ౧౪ ॥
    yasya syur vīta-saṅkalpāḥ
    [yasya syur veeta-sankalpaah]
    prāṇendriya-mano-dhiyām
    [praanendriya-mano-dhiyaam]
    vṛttayaḥ sa vinirmukto
    [vrittayah sa vinirmukto]
    deha-stho ’pi hi tad-guṇaiḥ
    [deha-stho ’pi hi tad-gunaih]
    Word-for-word
    yasya — of whom syuḥ — they are vīta — freed from saṅkalpāḥ — material desire prāṇa — of the vital energy indriya — the senses manaḥ — the mind dhiyām — and of intelligence vṛttayaḥ — the functions saḥ — such a person vinirmuktaḥ — completely freed deha — in the body sthaḥ — situated api — even though hi — certainly tat — of the body guṇaiḥ — from all of the qualities.

    from all of the qualities.
    A person is considered to be completely liberated from the gross and subtle material bodies when all the functions of his vital energy, senses, mind and intelligence are performed without material desire. Such a person, although situated within the body, is not entangled.
    Purport

    The material body and mind are subject to lamentation, illusion, hunger, lust, greed, insanity, frustration, etc., but one who remains active in this world without attachment is considered vinirmukta, or completely liberated. The vital energy, senses, mind and intelligence are purified when engaged in the devotional service of Lord Kṛṣṇa, as confirmed throughout Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    A person is considered to be completely liberated from the qualities of the body when all the functions of his prāṇa, senses, mind and intelligence are performed without material desire. How does the liberated person roam about? Freed from qualities of the body like lamentation and illusion, he roams about with the functions of prāṇa, senses, mind and intelligence free of desire.
    Personal Guidance
    Liberation is not bodily escape but acting through prāṇa, senses, mind, and intelligence without material desire — engaged in Kṛṣṇa's service, untouched within the body.
    Practical Applications
    • Before each task today, briefly offer the action to Kṛṣṇa so the senses and mind move without a private material agenda.
    • Notice one strong saṅkalpa (mental plan driven by personal desire) arising in you today and consciously redirect that energy into devotional service.
    • Refuse to wait for ideal external conditions to feel free — act in your present duties as one already vinirmukta, situated in the body but not entangled.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which functions of my body, senses, or intelligence am I still running on private saṅkalpa rather than on Kṛṣṇa's service?
    • Where do I confuse 'liberation' with withdrawing from activity, instead of with desireless engagement inside this very body?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • In one exam or assignment this week, work hard but offer the effort to Kṛṣṇa instead of treating the grade as your identity.
    • Notice the saṅkalpa behind your next social media post — who are you trying to impress? — and either drop the post or repurpose it in Kṛṣṇa's service.
    • When peer pressure pulls your senses toward something you know is material, stay engaged with friends but quietly refuse to let that desire steer your mind.
    householder
    • Take one routine family duty today — cooking, earning, parenting — and perform it as offered service, watching how vīta-saṅkalpa changes the quality of the act, not the act itself.
    • Before a financial or career decision this week, examine the saṅkalpa driving it; choose the option that keeps your intelligence engaged in Kṛṣṇa rather than in private enjoyment.
    • Train your children by example: let them see you act fully in the world while clearly not entangled by its outcomes, so they learn vinirmukta as a lived posture.
    senior
    • As the body weakens, deliberately practice acting through whatever senses and intelligence remain — chanting, hearing, advising — without the saṅkalpa of being noticed or remembered.
    • Mentor a younger devotee or family member today, transmitting not just instructions but the inner stance of deha-stho 'pi hi — present in the body, no longer entangled by it.
    • Loosen one attachment this week — a possession, a role, an expectation — and use the freed energy to deepen sādhana, demonstrating that liberation is available right inside this aging body.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Treating vinirmukta as a feeling of detachment to cultivate, rather than as the fruit of senses and mind actually engaged in bhakti.
    • Using 'I am not the body' as a slogan to avoid responsibilities, while material saṅkalpas continue running underneath.
    • Imagining liberation requires leaving family or work, instead of purifying the motive of action within one's current āśrama.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one area of your life where your senses are active but the underlying saṅkalpa is clearly material — how would devotional engagement change it without changing the outer act?
    • Do you find it easier to renounce activity or to purify the desire behind it? Where does the group disagree?
    • What is the difference, in your daily experience, between suppressing desires and being vīta-saṅkalpa as the verse describes?
    Role Play

    Two students pair up: one plays a sincere practitioner who has decided to quit a demanding job to 'become free from material entanglement'; the other plays a senior devotee who must respond using SB 11.11.14 — showing that vinirmukta is defined by desireless engagement of senses, mind, and intelligence, not by walking away from duty.

    Objections & Responses
    • If a liberated person still eats, works, and talks like everyone else, how is this any different from ordinary life with a spiritual label on top?
      The verse locates the difference precisely at the level of saṅkalpa — the inner motive driving prāṇa, senses, mind, and intelligence. Externally the activity may look identical, but when every function is offered in Kṛṣṇa's service rather than for personal enjoyment, the same body becomes a vehicle of liberation instead of bondage.
    • Modern psychology says desires are natural and healthy; isn't 'vīta-saṅkalpa' just repression dressed up as spirituality?
      Gauḍīya practice does not repress the faculties — it redirects them. The senses, mind, and intelligence remain fully alive and active; only the material saṅkalpa is replaced by devotional intent. That is why the verse calls such a person vinirmukta even while deha-stha — situated in the body, not numbed or suppressed.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.15

    ↗
    యస్యాత్మా హింస్యతే హింస్రైర్యేన కిఞ్చిద్ యద‍ృచ్ఛయా । అర్చ్యతే వా క్వ‍‍చిత్తత్ర న వ్యతిక్రియతే బుధ: ॥ ౧౫ ॥
    yasyātmā hiṁsyate hiṁsrair
    [yasyaatmaa himsyate himsrair]
    yena kiṣcid yadṛcchayā
    [yena kishchid yadrichchayaa]
    arcyate vā kvacit tatra
    [archyate vaa kvachit tatra]
    na vyatikriyate budhaḥ
    [na vyatikriyate budhah]
    Word-for-word
    yasya — of whom ātmā — the body hiṁsyate — is attacked hiṁsraiḥ — by sinful people or violent animals yena — by someone kiṣcit — somewhat yadṛcchayā — somehow or other arcyate — is worshiped vā — or kvacit — somewhere tatra — therein na — not vyatikriyate — is transformed or affected budhaḥ — one who is intelligent.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    yasyātmā hiṁsyate hiṁsrair

    one who is intelligent.
    Sometimes for no apparent reason one’s body is attacked by cruel people or violent animals. At other times and in other places, one will suddenly be offered great respect or worship. One who becomes neither angry when attacked nor satisfied when worshiped is actually intelligent.
    Purport

    If one does not become angry when attacked for no apparent reason, and if one does not become enlivened when glorified or worshiped, then one has passed the test of self-realization and is considered fixed in spiritual intelligence. Uddhava asked Lord Kṛṣṇa, kair vā jṣāyeta lakṣaṇaiḥ: by what symptoms can a self-realized person be recognized? Just as Lord Kṛṣṇa enlightened Arjuna, He now explains the same subject matter to Uddhava. In this verse the Lord describes symptoms by which it is very easy to recognize a saintly person, for a normal person becomes furious when criticized or attacked and overwhelmed with joy when glorified by others. There is a similar statement by Yājṣavalkya to the effect that one who is actually intelligent does not become angry though pricked with thorns and does not become satisfied at heart merely by being worshiped with auspicious paraphernalia such as sandalwood.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The liberated person, when attacked by violent creatures or when worshipped for some reason, is not affected by that. How is the liberated person to be recognized? Three verses give easily recognized qualities. The liberated person whose body is attacked by the wicked, by shoes or fists, or, without reason is worshipped with garland and sandalwood, is not disturbed. He does not become angry at the violent person or happy with the gentleman. Yājṣavalkya has said: yaḥ kaṇṭakair vitudati candanaiś ca vilimpati | akruddho’parituṣṭaś ca samas tasya ca tasya ca || The wise man, on being beaten with thorns or anointed with sandalwood, remains indifferent, not experiencing either angry or joy. Yājṣavalkya Smṛti 3.54
    Personal Guidance
    The budha is unmoved by sudden attack or sudden worship — neither anger nor elation moves the one fixed in the self.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice the next moment someone praises you and watch the lift in the chest — refuse to feed it; return to remembrance of Kṛṣṇa.
    • When attacked or criticized today without apparent cause, hold the body's flare of anger for three breaths before any reply.
    • At day's end, name one instance of praise and one of insult you received, and ask which one disturbed you more — that is the test.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which form of disturbance secretly rules me more — the sting of being attacked, or the warmth of being worshiped?
    • If a sudden insult and a sudden honor arrived today, what would each one reveal about where I am still identified with the body?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Watch the next social-media notification — likes on a post or a cutting comment — and notice which one pulls your mood more, then put the phone down for ten minutes.
    • When a friend group suddenly turns on you or suddenly elevates you, hold your reply for one full day and ask what the budha would say.
    • After your next exam result, whether top of the class or a failure, do your evening sādhana exactly the same as the day before.
    householder
    • When a colleague unfairly criticizes your work in a meeting, finish the day without venting at home — bring the disturbance to your morning japa instead.
    • When a relative praises you lavishly for your career or your children, refuse to repeat the praise to anyone else for a week.
    • Speak to your spouse tonight about one situation where family honor or insult is quietly running your decisions, and decide together what the budha would do.
    senior
    • When younger people show you sudden respect for your seniority, receive it without storing it — let the warmth pass through like wind through a screen.
    • When the body is attacked by illness or by neglect from those you cared for, sit with the hurt in front of your iṣṭa-deva rather than nursing it in private.
    • Mentor one younger devotee this week by showing them, with a story from your own life, how a single insult or a single honor used to derail your sādhana — and what changed.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Mistaking emotional numbness or suppression for the budha's equipoise — the verse asks for steadiness, not deadness.
    • Cultivating indifference to attack while still quietly drinking in praise, and calling that self-realization.
    • Using this verse to justify passivity in the face of real injustice toward others, when it speaks only about one's own reactions.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a recent moment when praise disturbed your sādhana more than criticism did — what did that reveal about your false ego?
    • Where in your life are you actively cultivating situations that bring you respect, and what would it cost you to stop?
    • Is the budha's evenness a feeling you wait for, or a discipline you practice in the moment — and what does the difference look like in daily life?
    Role Play

    Two students pair up. One enters the room and, for two minutes, alternates between sincere praise of the other's spiritual progress and sharp, unfair criticism of their character. The listener responds only after the two minutes, naming which one pulled them more strongly and why. Then they switch roles.

    Objections & Responses
    • Not reacting to attack or praise sounds like emotional repression — psychologically unhealthy, even dangerous.
      The verse is not asking you to suppress feeling but to stop identifying the self with the body that is being attacked or flattered. The budha still perceives clearly and may act protectively; what is gone is the inner heave of anger or elation, because the soul knows it is neither the body nor its reputation.
    • If I do not feel pleased when others worship me or angry when they harm me, how am I different from a stone?
      A stone is unmoved because it has no consciousness; the budha is unmoved because consciousness is fixed on Kṛṣṇa. The difference is the direction of the heart — full toward the Lord, and therefore unshaken by what merely touches the body.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.16

    ↗
    న స్తువీత న నిన్దేత కుర్వత: సాధ్వసాధు వా । వదతో గుణదోషాభ్యాం వర్జిత: సమద‍ృఙ్‍ముని: ॥ ౧౬ ॥
    na stuvīta na nindeta
    [na stuveeta na nindeta]
    kurvataḥ sādhv asādhu vā
    [kurvatah saadhv asaadhu vaa]
    vadato guṇa-doṣābhyāṁ
    [vadato guna-doshaabhyaam]
    varjitaḥ sama-dṛṅ muniḥ
    [varjitah sama-drin munih]
    Word-for-word
    na stuvīta — does not praise na nindeta — does not criticize kurvataḥ — those who are working sādhu — very nicely asādhu — very badly vā — or vadataḥ — those who are speaking guṇa-doṣābhyām — from good and bad qualities varjitaḥ — freed sama-dṛk — seeing things equally muniḥ — a saintly sage.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    na stuvīta na nindeta

    A saintly sage sees with equal vision and therefore is not affected by that which is materially good or bad. Indeed, although he observes others performing good and bad work and speaking properly and improperly, the sage does not praise or criticize anyone.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The sage seeing with equal vision is not affected by that which is materially good or bad. He does not praise or criticize persons who perform good or bad work or speak properly or improperly. He does not praise or condemn those who act or speak properly or improperly.
    Personal Guidance
    The sama-dṛṅ muni neither praises nor criticizes the good and bad acts of others — he sees with equal vision, free from guṇa-doṣa reactions.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice the next time you reach for praise or criticism of someone's behavior, and instead stay silent and offer the moment internally to Kṛṣṇa.
    • Refuse to participate in one piece of gossip today, even mild commentary on a coworker's or relative's choices.
    • Speak to one person whose conduct you usually judge and look past their guṇa-doṣa to the soul, treating them with equal vision.
    Reflection Questions
    • Whose 'bad work' do I most enjoy criticizing, and what does that habitual judgment reveal about my own un-surrendered attachments?
    • Where am I confusing the muni's sama-dṛṣṭi with cowardly silence or moral indifference, rather than freedom from guṇa-doṣa identification?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Refuse to post or repost any commentary today that ranks, ridicules, or praises another student's looks, grades, or relationships.
    • When your friend group starts dissecting a classmate's behavior, change the subject or quietly step out instead of adding your line.
    • Notice the urge to broadcast your own 'good work' for likes — and offer that one accomplishment silently to Kṛṣṇa instead.
    householder
    • Go one full day without criticizing your spouse's choices in front of the children, even when you privately disagree.
    • At work, when colleagues start praising or tearing down a third person who is not in the room, decline to add a sentence in either direction.
    • When raising your child, separate the necessary instruction of sva-dharma from the habit of running-commentary judgment — correct what must be corrected, and let the rest go.
    senior
    • Resist the elder's temptation to deliver verdicts on the lives of grown children, in-laws, and grandchildren — withhold the unsolicited praise as much as the unsolicited critique.
    • When recounting your life to a younger devotee, speak of events and lessons without rendering each old colleague a hero or villain.
    • In your daily japa and reading, train the mind to see the same eternal soul in the nurse, the neighbor, and the difficult relative — and let that vision be your legacy.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Mistaking spiritual neutrality for moral relativism — refusing to call wrong wrong even where sva-dharma plainly requires it of a guardian, teacher, or parent.
    • Withholding outward criticism while inwardly cataloguing every fault, so the tongue is quiet but the mind is louder than ever.
    • Using 'I am sama-dṛṅ' as a spiritual posture to look detached, when in fact one has simply lost interest in others' welfare.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one situation this week where you praised or criticized someone — was it from sama-dṛṣṭi, from guṇa-doṣa reaction, or from your own ahaṅkāra needing to feel superior?
    • When a parent corrects a child, a teacher corrects a student, or a devotee gently warns a friend — is that a violation of this verse, or is it a different kind of speech altogether? Defend your view.
    • Where in your devotional community do you see this verse genuinely lived, and where do you see it weaponized as an excuse to avoid responsibility?
    Role Play

    Two devotees are returning from a program where a third devotee behaved poorly — interrupting the speaker and boasting about his service. One devotee begins to vent and seeks agreement. The other devotee must respond in the mood of this verse: neither praising the venting, nor harshly rebuking the venter, nor joining in criticism of the absent third party. Play out the conversation for three minutes.

    Objections & Responses
    • If a sage never praises or criticizes, how does anyone in society learn right from wrong? This verse sounds like a recipe for passivity in the face of evil.
      The verse describes the inner vision of the muni who has transcended guṇa-doṣa identification with the body — he does not react with material approval or blame. It does not forbid a teacher, parent, or king from instructing those under their care according to sva-dharma. Kṛṣṇa Himself praises Arjuna and rebukes him within the same Gītā; the freedom here is from personal partisanship, not from dharmic instruction.
    • This sounds like the modern advice to 'not judge others' — just dressed up in Sanskrit. Why does it need a tradition?
      Modern non-judgment is usually rooted in the idea that there is no real standard, or that everyone's truth is equal. The muni's sama-dṛṣṭi rests on the opposite ground: he sees the same eternal soul, part and parcel of Kṛṣṇa, beneath every body, and knows that material good and bad are temporary movements of the guṇas. It is not relativism but a deeper realism, possible only because Kṛṣṇa and the jīva are real.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.17

    ↗
    న కుర్యాన్న వదేత్ కిఞ్చిన్న ధ్యాయేత్ సాధ్వసాధు వా । ఆత్మారామోఽనయా వృత్త్యా విచరేజ్జడవన్ముని: ॥ ౧౭ ॥
    na kuryān na vadet kiṣcin
    [na kuryaan na vadet kishchin]
    na dhyāyet sādhv asādhu vā
    [na dhyaayet saadhv asaadhu vaa]
    ātmārāmo ’nayā vṛttyā
    [aatmaaraamo ’nayaa vrittyaa]
    vicarej jaḍa-van muniḥ
    [vicharej jada-van munih]
    Word-for-word
    na kuryāt — should not do na vadet — should not speak kiṣcit — anything na dhyāyet — should not contemplate sādhu asādhu vā — either good or bad things ātma-ārāmaḥ — one who is taking pleasure in self-realization anayā — with this vṛttyā — lifestyle vicaret — he should wander jaḍa-vat — just like a stunted person muniḥ — a saintly sage.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    muniḥ
    a saintly sage.
    For the purpose of maintaining his body, a liberated sage should not act, speak or contemplate in terms of material good or bad. Rather, he should be detached in all material circumstances, and taking pleasure in self-realization, he should wander about engaged in this liberated lifestyle, appearing like a retarded person to outsiders.
    Purport

    According to Śrīla Jīva Gosvāmī, this verse describes a type of discipline recommended for the jṣāna-yogīs, who by intelligence try to understand that they are not their material bodies. One who is engaged in the devotional service of the Lord, however, accepts and rejects material things in terms of their usefulness in the loving service of Lord Kṛṣṇa. One who is trying to preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness should be seen as very intelligent and not jaḍa-vat, or stunted, as described here. Although a devotee of the Lord does not act, speak or contemplate for his personal sense gratification, he remains very busy working, speaking and meditating in the devotional service of the Lord. The devotee makes elaborate plans to engage all the fallen souls in Lord Kṛṣṇa’s service so they can be purified and go back home, back to Godhead. Merely rejecting material things is not perfect consciousness. One must see everything as the property of the Lord and meant for the Lord’s pleasure. One busily engaged in spreading the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement has no time to make material distinctions and thus automatically comes to the liberated platform.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    A liberated sage should not act, speak or contemplate in terms of material good or bad. Rather, taking pleasure in the self, he should wander about with this indifferent nature, appearing like a retarded person. With this nature, he appears to be a bound up person, not liberated.
    Personal Guidance
    True detachment is not stunted withdrawal but seeing every thing and word as Kṛṣṇa's property, engaged in His loving service.
    Practical Applications
    • Before judging anything today as material good or bad, pause and ask: how can this be engaged in Kṛṣṇa's service?
    • Speak less from personal preference and more from what serves the Lord's mission; if a remark would only inflate your ahaṅkāra, refuse it.
    • Take one possession or skill you privately consider yours and consciously offer it as the Lord's property meant for His pleasure.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where am I mistaking dullness or withdrawal for vairāgya, when Kṛṣṇa actually wants me busily engaged in His service?
    • Which material things am I still rejecting on principle that could, rightly seen, become instruments in the devotional service of the Lord?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before posting or reacting on social media today, ask whether the words serve Kṛṣṇa or just feed your ahaṅkāra — and if the latter, stay silent.
    • Stop sorting friends and classes into 'good for me' and 'bad for me'; instead, ask which of them you can engage, even slightly, in something Kṛṣṇa-conscious.
    • Refuse the cool-detached pose your peers admire; in your sādhana, choose busy devotional engagement over performative aloofness.
    householder
    • When making a family or career decision today, replace the question 'is this materially good or bad for us?' with 'how can this household and income be engaged in Kṛṣṇa's service?'
    • Notice the moments you go silent or withdraw from spouse or children calling it detachment, and instead speak and act in a way that engages them in devotion.
    • Treat the home, the salary, and the children's education explicitly as the Lord's property held in trust, and plan one concrete way this week to use them in His service.
    senior
    • Resist the temptation to retreat into silent disengagement as 'finally being detached'; instead, mentor younger devotees and engage your free time in active service.
    • Look at your possessions and savings and stop dividing them into things to keep and things to renounce; ask instead how each can be offered for the Lord's pleasure and His devotees' service.
    • When the body's decline tempts you to withdraw from speech and contemplation, deliberately speak Kṛṣṇa's glories and meditate on His service rather than going jaḍa-vat in the worldly sense.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Imitating the jaḍa-vat sage outwardly — speaking little, appearing aloof — while inwardly still calculating personal sense gratification.
    • Treating rejection of the material as the goal, instead of seeing everything as the Lord's property meant for His pleasure.
    • Using this verse to justify passivity in preaching, when Prabhupāda explicitly says the Kṛṣṇa conscious preacher is not stunted but most intelligent.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a recent moment when you went quiet or withdrew and called it detachment — was it really vairāgya, or avoidance dressed up in spiritual language?
    • Jīva Gosvāmī says this discipline is for the jṣāna-yogī; the bhakta engages everything in Kṛṣṇa's service. Where in your life have you confused the two paths, and at what cost?
    • If a devotee busy preaching looks far less serene than a silent renunciant, which one is closer to this verse's intent, and why does that bother us?
    Role Play

    A new student visits the temple and tells you they are giving up their job, friendships, and conversation to become 'detached like the sage in this verse.' Drawing on Prabhupāda's purport, show them in a five-minute exchange the difference between jaḍa-vat withdrawal and engaging everything as the Lord's property in devotional service.

    Objections & Responses
    • This verse sounds like spiritual bypassing — refusing to act, speak, or even think about good and bad is irresponsible in a world that needs engaged people.
      Prabhupāda anticipates exactly this misreading. The verse describes a jṣāna-yogī's discipline of not acting for personal sense gratification, not a license for passivity. The devotee remains very busy working, speaking, and meditating — but for the Lord's pleasure, not his own, which is the only truly responsible engagement.
    • If the liberated sage appears stunted to outsiders, isn't the devotee who preaches and organizes just clinging to worldly activity?
      Prabhupāda is explicit: one spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness should be seen as very intelligent, not jaḍa-vat. Merely rejecting things is not perfect consciousness; seeing everything as the Lord's property and engaging it in His service is. The preacher is more advanced, not less, because he has no time for material distinctions.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.11.18

    ↗
    శబ్దబ్రహ్మణి నిష్ణాతో న నిష్ణాయాత్ పరే యది । శ్రమస్తస్య శ్రమఫలో హ్యధేనుమివ రక్షత: ॥ ౧౮ ॥
    śabda-brahmaṇi niṣṇāto
    [shabda-brahmani nishnaato]
    na niṣṇāyāt pare yadi
    [na nishnaayaat pare yadi]
    śramas tasya śrama-phalo
    [shramas tasya shrama-phalo]
    hy adhenum iva rakṣataḥ
    [hy adhenum iva rakshatah]
    Word-for-word
    śabda-brahmaṇi — in the Vedic literature niṣṇātaḥ — expert through complete study na niṣṇāyāt — does not absorb the mind pare — in the Supreme yadi — if śramaḥ — labor tasya — his śrama — of great endeavor phalaḥ — the fruit hi — certainly adhenum — a cow that gives no milk iva — like rakṣataḥ — of one who is taking care of.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    śabda-brahmaṇi niṣṇāto

    in the Vedic literature
    niṣṇātaḥ
    expert through complete study
    na niṣṇāyāt
    does not absorb the mind

    pare
